184, October 3, 5 p.m.
I am informed that Admiral Takarabe, Minister
of Navy and a delegate to the London Conference, has
resigned his post as Minister following the treaty's
ratification. His successor is stated to be Admiral
Abo who was chief technical adviser to the Japanese
delegation.
Takarabe's resignation after the disposal of
the treaty has been rumored for some time and is not
a surprise. I do not anticipate serious political
consequences as a result of this incident.
FORBES

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Wushirjoh.
21.112/46
Sir:
I luve the honer to co. firl t telegram No. 184
of October Cr, 5 P.l., re ortine the resigation of
Adairal Takarabe as Minister of Marine und the appoint-
ment of Admiralt Abo te succeed him.
NEF
The resignation  f Ad iral Talarabe has been talked
of ever since the conclusion of the Treaty of London,
so that the announcement yesterday came as no surprise;
nevertheless, this is one of those events whioh periodi-
0
cally ocour. in Japanese politios, the origin and reasons
fof

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
for which seem to be beyond the power of the western
mind to appreciate. I am frank to confess that I
should be reluctant to attempt my own explanation of the
个
circumstanoes, and I believe that I could do no better
than to quote the explanation offered by the JIJI SHIMPO,
one of the leading papers of this city.
"When the invitation to the London Conference
was received last year, the Japunese Navy publibly
adopted the so-called three funda ental principles
as its utch-word . The Navy announced that these
princi les vere the unalterable conditions for
Japan's guidance at the Conference, nd, again,
that they represented the irreducible minimum
necessary for Japan's defenses. It constantly
sought to indoctrinate the Japanese people with
these ideas with the purpose of making them a part
of Japanese public opinion. Admiral Takarabe was
one of the most ardent of the propagandists and
he succeeded in engraving deeply in the minds of the
Japanese people the impression that, if these
principles were not adopted, the national defenses
would be jeopardized. When Admiral Takarabe left
Japan to attend the Conference as a delegate, he
made statements which allowed the Japanese people
to suppose he was resolved, if Japan's contentions
were not established, never to see Japan again.
These statements, therefore, persuaded the people
to

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
to entertain the hope that the three principles
would surely be established. It was unprecedented
for a nation, before the opening of a conference, to
publish the conditions under which it would attend
the conference and to indicate them as absolutely
their irreducible demands, and it was, therefore,
natural for the Japanese public to expect, in this
instance, that Japan's conditions would certainly
be established.  Our delegation then left for London,
having succeeded in seein the three principles
adopted by public opinion, and having assured them-
selves that they would have the support of the entire
nation. If the results of the Conference are examined,
one observes that a material concession was made
in respect of one of the three principles: a large
portion was broken off the corner of one of the
conditions which some days previously was declared
to be absolute and irred cible.
" The responsibility for the dispute which sub-
sequently broke out between the Government and the
Navy, which in turn brought about a serious reper-
cussion in political quarters, should be placed upon
Admiral Takarabe, the Chief propagandist of the"three
fundamental principles" doctrine. It is eminently
fitting that Admiral Takarabe should resign, and
the present was the best time for him to do it.
The

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.98)
The press is not unanimous, however, in thinking
that Admiral Takarabe chose an opportune time for re-
signing) As I have already pointed out in my despatch
500A15A3/1174
No. 16. Or October 2, 1930, the question of the funds to
be granted the Navy for its so-called replenishment pro-
gram is assuming serious proportions. The Tokyo NIChI
NICHI, also one of the leading pupers of the country,
expresses the opinion that Ad iral Takarabe would have
shown a much more comiendable sense of respo sibility,
if he had waited to resign until after some settlement
had been found of a vexatious problem, whieh he had no
little share in creatinc.
It is noted that this opinion is shared by a number
of prominent politicians .
The appointment of Admiral Abo to the Ministry of
Marine is also somewnat hari to unders and. Auiral
Abo, who it will be reme bered uttended the Conrereace
as a technical adviser, indulged in some very outspoken
criticism of the provisions of the Treaty before his return
to Japan. He has since kept himself very uen in the back-
ground, but he has not dissuised his dissatisfuction with
the terms obtained by Japan. In vie of this fact, therefore,
it would seem as though the Government had deliberately
sought to increase the difficulty of finding a solution
of the question of funds for the Navy by introducing into
the Cabinet an officer who is described as having the
loo per cent. confidence of the Navy. My own belief is
that

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.96)
-5-
A that the Government has not acted too hastily . It may
perhaps have chosen Admiral Abo for the reason that, if
he, who so thoroughly enjoys the confidence of the Navy.
can be induced to accept an amount less than the Navy now
demands, the Navy will not continue to offer opposition.
Or again, it is not unlikely that the Government came to
some general understanding with Admiral Abo before he
consented to join the Cabinet. In all events, I am quite
hopeful that his appointment will not, as some people
seem to think, necessarily complicate the problem further.
Respectfully yours,
hlamem Frhea
W. Caneron Forbes.

The Honorable
The Seoretary of State,
Washington.
148
Sir:
Confirming the Embassy's telegram No. 214, November
14, 1o A.M., I have the honor to state that Mr. Hamaguohi,
the Prime Minister, was shot on the morning of the l4th
instant, at Tokyo station, just as he was about to board
the train to attend the autumn military manoeuvres.
The Prime Minister was walking along the platform
towards his carriage, surrounded by a large number of
persons who had come to see him off, when a young man
emerged

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
emsrged from the orowd and, approaching within a few
yards or the Prime Minister, fired a shot whioh struok
Mr. Hamaguohi in the lower abdomen. Most of the persons
with the Prime Minister thought that the shot was the
aound of a flashlight, and it was not until the Prime
Minister collapsed that they realized that he had been
shot. The culprit was then seized by the police as he
was about to fire another shot. r. iamaguchi was carried
to the waiting room of the station and given emergenoy
treatment while his physicians were being summoned, and
as soon as the latter arrived they perceived that he was
seriously wounded. As he was bleeding very profusely
blood was transfused from the Premier's son. This
operation had some benefioial effeot and the Premier
was then immediately transported to the hospital of
the Tokyo Imperial University. An effort was made to
extract the bullet, and when this was unsuccessful the
physicians operated to prevent the development of com-
plications. The Prime Minister responded very favorably
to the operation, and the bulletins whioh have subsequently
been issued indioate that his condition has been steadily
improving. The last bulletin indicates that there is now
little likelihood of any serious relapse.
2
I was in Kobe when I heard of this regrettable oo-
ourrenee, and immediately telegraphed Baron Shidehara
expressions of my sympathy. The Pirst Secretary also
salled at the offieial residenee of the Prime Minister
to express his regrets, and on the following morning he
personally delivered to Baron Shidehare the message of
sympathy

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
3-
4
sympathy transmitted by your telegram No. 216, November
14, 1 P.M.. Baron Shidehare was extremely appreciative,
and requested the Embassy to express to you the warm thanks
of the Japanese Government.
The Cabinet met immediately after Mr.Hamaguohi was
removed to the hospital to disouss whether or not a prime
minister pro tempore should be appointed. Some of the
Ministers believed that, as it was obvious r. Hamaguohi
would be unable to attend to his duties for a considerable
period of time, the appointment should be made, while others
were of the opinion that there were no urgent matters of
state pending and that it would be advisable to be guided
by the progress of the Prime Minister's condition before
arriving at any decision. These two points of view could not
be reconoiled, but it was agreed to meet the following
morning, when the physicians could have had an opportunity
to observe the offeots of the operation.
The personal relationahips within the government party
are extremely complicated. The Minseito, like the Seiyukai,
is a fairly loose agglomeration of factions centering around
the more prominent men of the party. The two most important
factions, next to that of Mr. Hamaguohi, are those of Mr.
Adachi, Minister of the Interior, and Dr. Egi, Minister
of Railways. The high order of disoipline maintained within
the party does not allow the rivalry of these two leaders
to come conspiouously to the surface; but the success with
whieh Mr. Adachi conducted the last eleotion campaign,
whioh

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
whieh resulted in a victory for the Minseito, and the
adroitneas with whioh Dr. Egi, who is Mr. Hamaguohi's
"sontaet man", led Mr. Hamaguohi through the dispute over the
London Naval Treaty, make these two the outstanding contenders
for succession to Mr. Hamaguohi.
Neither of these men were anxious to have the other
replace Mr. Hamaguohi temporarily, as in the event of his
death the inoumbent would naturally have priority in seleotion,
while some disturbanoe would inevitably arise in the party if
either received preferment before the party had fixed in some
way their relative position. Dr. Egi's influence is not so
great perhaps as that of Mr. Adaohi, and the probabilities
are that, if the Cabinet had to ohose between the two it would
have voted for Mr. Adaohi. These oiroumstanoes undoubtedly
explain the fact that Mr. Adachi's faction favored the ap-
pointment of a prime minister pro tempore, while Dr. Egi led
the group which favored delay.
After the assassination of Prime Minister Hara in l92l,
the Cabinet nominated as prime minister pro tempore Count
Uohida, the Minister for Foreign Affairs, as he was the senior
in point of court rank. Baron Shidehara has, after Mr.
OBF
Hamaguohi, the highest oourt rank . He is a member of the
Minseito, but he is not a party politician and had little
influence in the organization beyond the influence whioh his
character and ability command. His seleotion as temporary
head of the Government would not disturb the balance of
power within the party, and, furthermore, it would have the
sanetion of precedent. It was presumably with these con-
sideretions in mind that Dr. Egi is reported to hare sounded
out.

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Î out Baron Shidehara and secured his consent to replaoing
Mr. Hamaguohi temporarily. I understand on very good au-
thority that he obtained the approval of Mr. Wakatsuki, one
of the elders of the Minseito, and immediately despatohed
a dooument to the temporary offices of the Cabinet at the
headquarters of the Emperor near the manoeuvre grounds re-
questing the appointment of Baron Shidehara with instruc-
tions to present it to the Emperor for signature as soon
as telegraphic orders were received from Tokyo.
When the Cabinet met again on Saturday morning, the
15th, Dr. Egi reported to his colleagues the actions he
had taken, and the Cabinet deoided to nominate Baron Shidehara
without further delay.
It would be reasonable to assume that Baron Shidehara
has undertaken this additional office on a reasonable ex-
pectation of Mr. Hamaguchi being completely recovered by
the middle of January, when the Diet reopens after the New
Year recess . If Mr. Hamaguchi should suffer a relapse or
if his recovery should be delayed beyond this date, some
politioal disturbance may evidence itself.
A censorship has been placed upon the publication of
any information regarding the assassin or the motives of the
crime; but before it was put into effect, the press reported
thet the culprit belonged to an organization ostensibly
dedicated to the support of the Emperor Cult and other forms
of conservatism. There are many such organizations, the
Black Dragon Society, the National Essence Society, the
Ratriotie Society, and so on; but they have a common
fountainhead in one Mitsuru Toyama. These sooieties ap-
parently work under his direotion, and are usually mobilized
whenever

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
whenever the Government can be acoused of betreying
national interests. For example, they were partieularly
active in making demonstrations against the Government
during the Naval Conference. Those belonging to these
societies subsist on subscriptions "extorted from large
corporations and political organizations, and it is a
notorious faot that they can be hired for illegal pur-
poses; yet the authorities seem to be disinclined to
suppress them, perhaps on account of a gloss of patriotism
with whioh they cover their acts. The oulprit in this
instanoe is supposed to belong to the Patriotio Society,
whioh is an offshoot of the Black Dragon Sooiety; and it
is not at all improbable that he was inoited by persons
dissatisfied with the Naval Treaty to do away with the
person instrumental in securing Japan's adherence to it.
In faot, the surprising thing is that no attack was made
on the Prime Minister long ago.
This attempt upon the life of Mr. Hamaguchi has called
forth expressions of horror from all sides. A number of
parallels were drawn between the assassination of. Mr. Hara
and the attempt upon Mr. Hamaguohi; and it is interesting
to note that while the denunoiations of Mr. Hara's assail-
ant were relieved by oriticiams of Mr. Hara, the attaok
on Mr. Hamaguchi is unequivocally and universally condemned.
The Tokyo AsAHI's editorial quoted below closely refleots
opinion heard on all sides:
" The aonduet of Mr. Hamaguohi, both as a
publie man and as a private individual, is above
reproash. Not only is he a statesman or unusual
honesty and oharacter, but it would be impossible
to find one better fitted to carry out a poliey
of retrenshment, whieh is imperative ir the coustry
is to be taken out of its present dirrioult sttmation.
Furthermore,

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
furthermore, the faet that he has aiways pursued
the course whioh publio opinion believes to be the
proper oourse is amply demonstrated by the events
of the past summer, when Mr. Hamaguohi collided with
the Privy Counoil over the Naval Treaty."
Respeotfully yours,
W. Cameron Forbes.

Sagoya Tomeo, the would-be assassin, upon
examination soon after his arrest, denied all intent
to urder the romier but admitted that he was of
the opinion that the business depression and in-
oreasing unemployment vere due to the blundering
polioy of the present Government. As the examina-
tion progressed, however, it was usoertained that
the youth oonceived the plot as early as last May,
but was unable to find an opportunity until November
l4th, the date of the Premier's intended departure
for the Military Manoeuvres. The revolver with which

Baron Wakatsuki submitted the resi nation of the
Cabinet to the Emperor at 5:30 this afternoon. Resig-
nation was due to the instructions of Adachi, Minister
of Home Affairs, that a coalition cabinet of Minseito
and Seiyukai members ought to be formed (see my telegram
323
No. 232
November 28). The present situation is the result of
political intrigue and not of policies nor actions of
the Government nor, according to all reports we have
received, due to anything connected with the Manchurian
situation.
The successor of Baron Wakatsuki will not be known
until tomorrow or later. The probabilities seem to
be that either Baron Wakatsuki or M. Inukai, President
of the Seiyukai the opposition party, will be called
upon to form the new ministry. The former is prominently

Home Minister in view of the Faot that he is an elder of
the Minseito may be antagonistic to Mr. Takahashi the
Minister of Finance who is a Seiyukai spokesman. It is
further feared that the alliance of Mr. Hajoyama Minister
of Education and Mr. Minami Minister of Communications
may oppose that of Mr. Nagasi Overseas Minister and Mr. Goto
Minister of Agriculture and Dendrology.
The financial circle is disappointed at the
cabinet errangement. A view prevai!s that Visoount Baito
has failed to reach perfect understanding with the Seiyukai
and the Minseito and so his cabinet has been formed with
men of the second olass. The financial circle does not
consider that the new government is as efficient as
desired.
The military clique also does not consider the
new cabinet to be in a line of suoh coalition as it first
desired. The olique is however satisfied under the
impression that it is a non-party cabinet at a transition."

Despatoh No. 635 of June 2, 1932,
from the Embassy at Tokyo deals with
the new Saito Cabinet.
The young army group, whioh it is
believed is working toward a military
diotatorship, desired a super-party
Cabinet while the politioal parties and
the Genro desired a Cabinet along
constitutional lines. A compromise was
reaohed by the inolusion in the Cabinet
of seven party men and four independents.
Most of the party men are not strong
men, however, and the Cabinet appears
to be in faot a super-party Cabinet
dominated by General Araki.
There are four men of fairly high
calibre in the Cabinet and eight of
medioore attainments. The strongest
is General Araki, who, as representative
of the young army group, may be in a
position to diotate the policies of the
Cabinet. The other three are the
Finance Minister, Takahashi, who was
Finance Minister in the preceding
Seiyukai Cabinet, the Home Minister,
Baron Yamamoto, an experienoed finanoier
who served as Finanoe Minister some time
ago in a Minseito Cabinet, and the

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
Prime Minister, Saito, who has no
outstanding eapabilities but is easy-
geing, inelined to compromise, and
a non-partisan political leader.
The Japanese were apparently dis-
appointed in the new Cabinet sinoe it
had been expeoted that it would be
composed of the leading statesmen
regardless of olass or party in order
to face the present grave issues.
These issues are:
(1) social and agrarian unrest
the basio cause of whioh is the tremen-
dous growth in power and influenoe of
suoh family groups as the Mitsuis, the
Iwasakis and the Sumitomos, with their
tendenoy to absorb the wealth of the
nation and to control, allegedly by
corrupt praotices, the various branohes
of the Government. The Army resenting
this, wishes to revert to feudal days
or the Meiji era, when the economio
polioies of the country were not con-
trolled by the meroantile olass. In
this they are backed by all the re-
actionary groups and the radical
element among the farmers. It seems
unlikely that any cabinet could cor-
reot the basic causes of this unrest.
(2) National finance. Japan is
unable to balance its budget and must
resort to loans. There is also a
strong movement toward currenoy infla-

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
tien, in erder to decrease the value
of the yen, relieve the credit situa-
tiem, and deorease the value of the
enormous debts with whioh the farmers,
industrialists, merohants and shipping
cempanies are burdened.
(3) Purification of politics.
The people have lost all oonfidence in
and respect for politioal parties
and reforms must be made.
(4) The report of the League
Commission. A conviotion is gaining
ground in Japan that the report of
the Commission will not meet Japan's
wishes and that it will be necessary to
decide whether or not definitely to defy
the League and the Powers whioh will
support the League.
(5) Relations with the Soviets
being strained, it may be necessary
to dooide upon the attitude to be
takon.
It is presumed that in these
last two questions, the opinion of the
army group, as represented by General
Araki, will to a large extent prevail.
In the Cabinet there may be dis-
union because of the different
politioal faotions represented therein.

FILD
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that there is a large
measure of opinion among the Japenese that the newly
formed Saito Cabinet is merely a stop-gap Cabinet which
will neither meet the urgent needs of the country nor
have a long term of office.
Factors in the Formation of the Cabinet.
After the assassination of former Premier Inukai
by a group of young Army and Navy officers, it was
generally

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
generally reported that any Cabinet which would be formed
would have to be acceptable to the Army. The radical
younger group in the Army desires elimination of corrupt
party politios, lessening or abolition of the influence
in government of the great financial interests, reform
of economic policies in favor of the masses, and a stronger
policy toward China, regardless of the opinion of the world.
This group, which it is believed is working toward a military
dictatorship- a revival of the Shogunate- insisted upon a
super-party Cabinet, to ti e the nation over the present
financial and political crisis. At the same time, the
political parties, supported by Prince Saionji, who is a
staunch upholder of constitutional government, insisted
that a Cabinet be formed somewhat along constitutional
lines, with members from the leading olitical groups.
The public was interested principally in the formation of
a Cabinet vwhich would make some rositive move toward
alleviation of the economic distress, which would quiet
the social unrest, and which would make an attempt to
eliminate political corruption. These various factors
resulted in the formation of the Saito Cabinet, a com-
promise Cabinet more or less acceptable to all parties
but completely fulfilling the wishes of none.
Composition of the Cabinet.
The Premier and Foreign Minister pro tem, Admiral
Viscount Makoto Saito, is a commoner by birth, a naval
offioer by profession and a statesman and administrator
by choice. He became known to the world through his very
able administration of Korea after the passive rebellion
there

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
there in l919. He has no outstanding capabilities, but
he is affaule, easy-going and inclined to compromise. Ho
is noted for his integrity and is described as being "unadle
to do anything badly". He is affiliated with no political
party, but for years has been one of the non-partisan
political leaders of the country . He was ac eptable to
the Army group because of his lack of affiliation with
the political parties and because of his supposed amenability
to the demands of the Army group.
The Finance Minister, Mr. Korekiyo Takahashi, was taken
over into the present Cabinet from the previous Inukai Cabinet,
largely in order that he might carry out the financial
policies which he had inaugurated and which it was thought
advisable to continue. He is a man of c nsiderable experience
both in finance and in politics, having served for years in
governmental banks and having been Finance Minister twice.
Although a Seiyukai man( President ad interim once), he is
distinctly not a party man, is independent and fearless in
thought, and is inolined to place the nation's interests
above the party's interests. Hence he was acceptable to
those endeavoring to form a national or super-party Cabinet.
The Home Minister, Baron Tatsuo Yamamoto, is a publio
man and financier of long experience. He served for years
in governmental banks, rising to the highest positions, and
served one term as Finance Minister and twice as Minister of
Agriculture and Forestry. He was originally a member of the
Seiyukai but on a split in that party he became a member of
the Minseito, where he has been acting as adviser. He is not
a strong party man and is said to have more friends in the
seiyukai

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Seiyukai than in the Minseito. He retired from active
publio life some ten years ago and was reluotant to join
the Saito Cabinet. He was persuaded, however, on the
ground that a super-party Cabinet could not be formed
without his help and that it was imperative to form a
Cabinet as soon as possible to avoid the possi ble collapse
of parliamentary government in Japan.
The Minister of War, Lieutenant-General Sadao Araki,
is a man who has come into the public eye in the last few
months. He is the enigma, and threatens to be the storm-
center, of the present Cabinet. He is of samurai blood
and an Army officer by profession. After working up to
the higher grades of his service, he became Minister of
War in the Inukai Cabinet. He is a member of the Koku-
honsha, a s mewhat radical and at the same time intensely
nationalistic party which includes the younger Army group.
While Minister of ar in the Inukai Cabinet and campaignig
for the Kokuhonsha, he made certain remarkably frank speeches
regarding Japan's intentions in Manchuria. When Premier
Inukai was assassinated by a group of young Army officers,
General Araki, in accordance with Japanese custom, accepted
responsibility and resigned. Later, when the Saito Cabinet
was being formed, he reconsidered his resignation and re-
mained in the new Cabinet. This action was unumual in view
of the Japanese conception of proper conduct in such cases,
and he is being severely criticized throughout the country.
In reply to the criticism, he intimates that he has certain
duties which he must perform, despite all criticimm, and
compares himselr with the forty-seven ronin, who led degraded
11ves

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
' lives and braved adverse criticism in order eventually
to attain thetr purpose. He does not erplain the end to
whioh he is working. He is probably the strongest man in
the Cabinet, and as the representative of the young Army
group, may be in a position to dictate the policies of the
Cabinet.
These four( Saito, Takahashi, Yamamoto and Araki) are
the outstaning men of the Cabinet. The others are lieu-
tenants of the leaders or representatives of parties or
departments.
The Minister of the Navy, Admiral Keisuke Okada, is a
man of considerable administrative ability but of no special
characteristics. He served as Minister of the Navy once
before, in 1927, in the Tanaka Cabinet.
The Minister of Commerce and Industry, Baron Kumakichi
Nakajima, is a peer by birth. He has had some experience
in business, having veen an official in various banks and
steamship, electric, trust and insurance companies. He is
a member of the Iouse of Peers and of the Koseikai( an
influential group in the House of Peers), whom he represents
in the Cabinet.
The Minister of Comnunications, Mr. Hiroshi Minami, is
a member of the Seiyukai and has held various offices, in-
cluding that of Governer General of Formosa, under Seiyukai
Cabinets. He is a strong party man and is one of the re-
presentatives of the Seiyukai in the Saito Cabinet.
The Minister of Railways, Mr. Chuzo Mitsuchi, is a
member of the Seiyukai, but he is not a strong party man
and is rather a lieutenant of Mr. Takahashi, the Finance
Minister, than a representative of the Seiyukai. He has long
been a member of the Lower House and has twice before held
Cabinet

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Cabinet positions, as Minister of Education in l927 and
as Finance Minister in 1929.
The Minister of Agrioulture and Forestry, Mr. Fumio
Goto, is a lieutenant of Baron Yamamoto, the Home Minister,
who insisted, as a condition to his own participation in
the Cabinet, upon the a pointment of Goto as a Minister or
Vice Minister. e is a member of the Minseito but is not
a strong party man. He has been in government service since
1908, in various subordinate positions.
The Minister of Education, Mr. Ichiro Hatoyama, was
carried over from the Inukai Cabinet, where he held the
same portfolio. He is a member of the Seiyukai, a strong
party man and a lieutenant of Dr. Suzuki, the President of
the Seiyukai. He has been a politician since his graduation
from law school and has been repeatedly elected to the Lower
House.
The Minister of Justice, Mr. Matsukichi Koyama, is a
prominent jurist who has served as procurator and judge
in various courts in Japan since l894. Before appointment
to a Cabinet position he was Procurator General and Dean
of Hosei University( a law school). He has no party af-
filiations.
The Overseas Minister, r. Ryutaro Nagai, is a member
of the Minseito, has been elected to the Lower House several
times on that tioket, has served as Parliamentary Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs and at the time of his appoint-
ment to the Cabinet was Chief Secretary of the Minseito.
He is a strong party man, and is regarded in some quarters
as likely to be a factor of importance in political life
in Japan.
The

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
The Saito Cabinet is thus composed of four members
of the Seiyukai, three of the Minseito and one of the
Peers, with four independents. Most of the party men,
however, are not strong party men, and the Cabinet would
appear to be in fact a super-party Cabinet, dominated
by General Araki.
Reaction to the Hew Cabinet.
The general reaction among the Japanese to the new Cabinet
is one of disappointient. It was uxpected that, with the
many grave issues facing the nation, a very strong Cabinet
composed of the leading statesmen of the nation, regardless
of class or party affiliations, would be formed. Instead,
a Cabinet containing four men of fairly high calibre and
eight men of mediocre attainments has been presented to the
nation. The newspaper ditorials do not indicate any con-
siderable enthusiasm over the new Cabinet. Financial circles
are disappointed, as they kmow from past experience that
Takahashi will be disinclined to take strong positive measures
for relief of the financial situation. The Seiyukai, which
controls the Lower House at present, does not give any indica-
tion of wholehearted support of the Cabinet and may make
trouble in the Diet. The Army circles are non-committal
but threaten to make trouble if the Cabinet proves to be
amenable to the dictates of the politioal parties rather
than to those of the Army.
Problems before the Saito Cabinet.
The Saito Cabinet faces the largest group of urgent
problems faced by any Cabinet in recent years. Among the
domestio problems the most important is the social and
agrarian

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
agrarian unrest. The people of Japan have been dis-
illusioned in regard to parliamentary government, there
has been a steady decline of discipline in the Army, Navy
and the schools, and the economic depression and increasing
unemployment have intensified the discontent. The reactionary
or extreme nationalist movement has a curious tendency to
combine, in thought at least, with the extreme left or radical
movement, and to favor the abolition of large private fortunes
with their tendency to corrupt the government, and to favor
a wider distribution of wealth anong the masses. The basic
cause of the unrest is the tremendous growth in power and
influence of such family groups as the Mitsuis, the Iwasakis
and the Sumitomos, with their tendency to absorb the wealth
of the nation and to control, allegedly by corrupt practices,
the various branches of the Government. The Army resents the
growth of this money power and wishes to revert to the feudal
days, or even to those of the Meiji era, when the economic
policies of the country were not controlled by the mercantile
olass. In this movement they have the backing of all re-
actionary groups and of the radical element among the farmers,
who are hard pressed at present by the eopnomic depression.
It seems unlikely that any Cabinet which could be formed
would be able to do much to oorrect the basic causes of the
unrest, especially as the depression is not confined to Japan
but is world-wide.
Another important domestic problem is that of national
finance., Japan, like many other countries, is unable to
balance its budget and must resort to loans. There is also
a strong movement toward currency inflation, in order to
depreciate the yen, relieve the credit situation, and decrease
the value of the enormous debts with which the farmers,
industrialists,

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
industrialists, merchants and shipping companies are
burdened.
A third important domestic problem, which is to
some extent included in the first, consists of the
purification of politics. The people of the country have
lost all confidence in and respect for political parties
and reforms must be instituted if the parliamentary system
is to regain its lost prestige.
In foreign affairs, the Cabinet must meet, within
a short time, the report of the Comnission of the eague
on the Manchurian question. A conviction is gaining ground
in Japan that the report of the Comaission will not meet
Japan's wishes and that it will be necessary to decide
whether or not definitely to defy the League and the Powers
which will support the League. Relations with the Soviets
are also somewhat strained and it may be necessary to
decide upon the attitude which will be taken in regard to
this question. It is presumed that in both of these questions,
the opinion of the Army group, as represented by General
Araki, will to a large extent prevail.
Within the Cabinet itself there may be disunion, as the
Premier will have to reconcile the views of Ministers of
the different political factions. The Minseito has promised
its support to the Cabinet, but the Seiyukai, which controls
a clear majority in the Lewer House, has not indicated any
desire to give its whole-hearted support to the new Cabinet.
Within a day of the inauguration of the Cabinet, dissension
between the members representing the political parties over
the distribution of the appointments of vice-ministers had
arisen.
Taoo

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.97)
-10-
Face to face with these numerous and pressing
problems, some of which seem incapable of any immediate
solution, it appears unlikely that the Saito Cabinet will
be able to maintain itself.
Respectfully yours,
Eduin P huilh
Edwin L. Neville,
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.
Embassy's File No. 800.Japan

OKH.
Tokyo's despatch No. 254 of
January 13, 1933, reports the
resignation of Okada, Minister of
Navy, and the appointment of Osumi
to the post. The resignation was
due to ill health and the change
was merely routine. This brief
despatch should be read in full.
NES:KC

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of the Navy are not dictated by the Minister of the
Navy alone, the latter being regarded in a measure as
the representative of the Navy in the civilian government.
Naval policies are therefore not affected to any great
extent by a change of minister. The position is of
course not without great authority, but consent to such
appointment, as in uny other matter of importance affecting
the Navy, must first be obtained from the leaders of the
Navy.
From one point of viev. it is unfortunate that the change
occurred just when it did. Admiral Okada would have reached
the age of retirement on the 20th of this month. Had
he remained in office beyond that time a new precedent
would have been set in the interpretation of the ordinance
requiring the post of Minister of the Navy to be occupied
by an admiral. Thus far the ordinance has been interpreted
as meaning an admiral(or general for the War Department)
in acfive service, although legally no distinction is
made. The resignation of Admiral Okada is, therefore,
regarded as helping to establish the precedent that the
Navy portfolio shall be held only by an admiral on the
active list. As such it is regarded as a victory for the
military, making their independent position even stronger.
It is notable that the Premier, himself an admiral, was most
active in the effort to persuade Admiral Okada to remain
in office before his illness took a serious turn, in-
dicating that Premier Saito has liberal views in regard to
the Navy's relationship to the civil government.
Ther

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
1/
There is herewith transmitted a report on the
form "confidential biographic data" in regard to Admiral
Osumi. The latter is no stranger in polities, having
been Minister of Navy in the Inukai Cabinet and previously
Vice Minister of Navy. At the time of the controversy over
the London Naval Treaty, it was he who wa: credited vith
bringing the opoosing factions into agreement. He is a
popular and able officer, possessing qualities of states-
manship unusual cmong men of the military caste.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

In the opinion of well-infor ed observers Uchida's resig
nation was due not to any dissatisraction on his part with
the general policy of the present govornment, but to reasons
of health and unwillingnoss longer to bear the burdens of
office. It seems probable that there may be a general re-
arrangement of the Cabinet in the relatively near future,
and that the Minister for Foreign Affairs probably is only
anticipating his retirement by a few months.
Hirota is a diplomat of wide experienoe. He is not
notably a strong man, but has a reputation for conciliatory
BF
conduct. In his youth he was identified with a "patriotic"
society sponsored by Toyama. Although he has not been active
commented on the matter. It seems probable that his appoint-
ment is due to there being nothing against him. His service
in

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)

2-141, From Toky0, Sept.15, 3 pom.
in Russia gives him first hand familiarity with the ques-
tions at issue with the Soviets. As he is not an alarmist
it may be assumed that he is regarded as a safe man in the
way of a stop-gap, or he may be on trial to see if he will
be acceptable in any rearrangement of the Cabinet that may
later take place.

CHANGE UY RINISTERS FOR FOREIGN AFFAIRS.
SP 20 1933
Mr. Koki Hirote has been appointed Japanese Minister
0BF
0
for Foreign Affairs, succeeding Count Uchida.
002
Mr. Hirota, who is fifty-five years of age, entered
894.002/219
the Foreign Offioe in 1906 after graduation from the
894.
College of Law of the Tokyo Imperial University. After
serving at various posts abroad, inoluding an assignment
as First Seoretary at Washington from 1918 to 1921, in
1923 he was appointed Chief of the Bureau of European and
American Affairs in the Foreign Ofrice. While ocoupying
this post he was to a large degree responsible for the
successful conclusion of the negotiations which reestab-
lished relations between Japan and Russia in May l924. In
November 1926, he was appointed Minister to The Hague. In
SEP
stated that Mr. Hirota "is looked upon as one of the most
1933
able of the younger men in the Foreign Service"; and, disous-
sing the interest of Japan in the Duteh East Indies, added,
"the transfer of an officer of the calibre of Mr. Hirota to
The Hague may well be construed as indicating the intention
of the Japanese Government to pursue its econemie pelieies as
vigorously as possible"; and, commenting en Mr. Hirota's
sentiments
<

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.95)
- 8 -
 sentiments towards the United States, said, "Mr. Hirete,
with whem members of my staff are in pleasant persomal and
official relations, has always shown himself to be friemdly
disposed to the United States and to Amerieans".
In 1930 Mr. Hirota was transferred to Mosoow as Ambas-
sador. At this time the relations between Japan and Russia
were not satisfactory. Trouble had arisen over the question
of fishing concessions claimed by Japanese in Russian waters,
Russians were accused of instigating seditious and communistio
movements in Japan, while Japanese were being arrested in
Russia for engaging in illegal exchange transactions. The
American Ambassador at Tokyo, Mr. Cameron Forbes, when report-
ing to the Department the appointment of Mr. Hirota to Moseow,
said: "It would seem that the Japanese Government has taken
pains to find a capable man for this difficult post in the
hope that a solution or the various questions pending between
the two countries may be found," and that "Mr. Hirota is a
man of excellent reputation and wide experience."
A
Mr. Hirota was relieved of his post at Mosoow in 19s8
and has sinee been "en disponibilitó". He was tenth in
seniority on the list of Ambassadors, being junior to both
Tらuい
Mr. Matsudaira, Ambassador at London, and Mr. Debuthi, Ambas-
sador at Washington, both of whom have exceeded the usual
term at these posts. Mr. Hirota is also junior to three other
ambassadors who are also on "en disponibilité" and whe would
thus have been available to succeed Count Uchida.
r. Grow

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.95)
- 8 -
Mr. Grew has not commented as yet on the recent change
of Foreign Ministers. A despatoh from the Tokyo eorrespemdent
of the NEW YORk TIEs, who is unusually well informed, states
that Mr. Hirota is extremely nationalistic.
2
The possibility that Mr. Hirota was appointed because he
is more moderate and liberal than either Messrs. Matsudaira
or Debuehi may be dismissed. The possibility that he is more
nationalistic than Ambassador Obata, who is now in Japan with-
out a post, is unlikely, as the vigorous part taken by Mr.
Obata, when Chargé d'Affaires at Peiping during the so-called
Twenty-one Demands, will be remembered. Mr. Hirota has
hitherto had the reputation of being a liberal and progressive
diplomat, and it is therefore very difficult to believe that
he has been selected from a point far down the list of Ambas-
sadors because of being an extreme nationalist.
It is impossible to speak dogmatically on the reasons
for Mr. Hirota's selection, as virtually nothing has been
heard of him during the past year. In view of all the attend-
ant sireumstanees, it is believed that Mr. Hirota stands some-
where between the two opposite groups of Japanese diplomats,
and it may be surmised that his appointment was based in part
on his proven ability and in part on his exoeptional knowledge
of conditions in Russia, rather than on political sentiments
and beliefs. If the latter were the case there were available
more suitable candidates. At the same time, conditions new
existing in Japan make it certain that he enjoys the confidenee
 of the military party.
ao

therefore impossible to say whether Count Uohida was foreed
out of offioe by the military or was obliged to resign on
account of ill health, the latter being the ostensible reason
for his resignation.

Washington Herald

NEW OFFICIAL
WILL ASSUME
DUTIES TODAY
Ill Health Reason for Retire-
ment; Served as Envoy to
U. S.; Was Ex-Premier
TOKYO. Sept. 14 (U.P.).—Ya
suya Uchida, foreign minister of
Japan, resigned today.
Ill health was given as the
reason.
Koki Hirota, until recently Jap-
anese Ambassador to the Soviet
Government, was to be installed
as foreign minister at 1:30 p. m.
today. Emperor Hirohito formally
will install Hirota in a ceremony
at Hayama palace.
RESIGNS SUDDENLY
Uchida's resignation came sud-
denly, after he had guided Japan's
foreign policy during one of the
most difficult periods in the long
history of the empire.
He became foreign minister in
the "super party" cabinet formed
by Premier Makoto Saito, and
was in charge of Japan's foreign
policy when Japan withdrew from
the League of Nations after that
body's criticism of the Manchu-
rian incident.
The resigned foreign minister
had served as minister to China,
ambassador to Austria, and am-
bassador to the United States. He
was forelgn minister in the cabi-
net of Viscount Sajonji. He
served the Hara government as
foreign minister in 1918 and acted
temporarily as premier in 1921.

Set Up in North China
PRIPING. China. Sept.
18
(U.P.).—The Chinese are willing

Japan 'Buffer State'
Set Up in North China
PEIPING, China, Sept. 13
(U.P.).—The Chinese are willing
to admit today that Japan has
established a "buffer state" in
North China itself, below the
Great Wall, between Peiping and
their puppet nation of Man-
choukuo.
Despite protests to the League
of Nations at Japan's encroach-
ment onto the continent of Asia.
the Chine.- have been forced to
recognize that the Tokyo govern-
ment's seizures of China territory
include a slice of China proper
about the size of the State of
New Jersey.
CHINESE FLAG FLIES
The Chinese Kuomintang flag
still flies over North China. but
it is admitted the Chinese of-
ficials are controlled by the Jap-
anese in that area above Tientsin
and Peiping. just as they are in
Manchoukuo.
This development is the latest
move in Japan's bold seizure of
Manchuria, Jehol Province and
this sphere of influence below the
Wall, which began with the Man-
churian campaign just two years
ago this month.
The Chinese police patrolling
this buffer area are former sol-
diers of Gen. Li Chi-Chun, a
Chinese chieftain who aided the
Japanese and Manchoukuo forces
in the advance which brought
the Japanese to within striking dis-
tance of Peiping. General Li
last April raised the old 5-bar
flag in rebellion against the Nan-
king government. Now, however.
he is recognised as a Chinese of-
ficial who receives his money
from China-but his orders from
Manchoukuo.
NATIONAL DISGRACE'
Under the terms of the truce
last May, the Chinese are not
permitted to maintain even de-
fense forces in this "buffer
The truce was deseribed in the
Chen Pao, influential Chinese
newspaper in Peiping. as "a na-
tlonal humiliation and disgrace."
It added:

the imcident of September 18,

Several radically dif erent lypotheses are advanced
in various quarters to accourt for the resignation yes-
terday of General Ara as  in ster of Jar, none of which

0

physical ability to carry on his work for some time to
come. The following factors and conjectures are, however,
being advanced in various quarters.
One. There are those who hold that the saner elements
in the Government, realizing the dangerous position into
which Japan is drifting, have taken advantage of Araki's
illness to insist on his removal as the symbol of a
policy of military aggression and that as a result we
may expect to see an important reoriontation of policy.

indignation at the size of the military budget. It is
said that Wakatsuki and Suzuki recently threatened em-
barrassing interpellations in the Diet which would have
placed the army in an unfavorable light before the public
and that they agreed to refrain only on the basis of
Araki's rctirement.
Two. There has boen dissatisfa tion among the young-
er officers in the army at leing hidored from promotion
owing to Araki's tendency to retain the older generals.
General Hayashi, the new Minister of War, is a leader
of troops rather than a staff officer and is more likely
to retire the older generals and advance the youner men
with whom he is popular.
Three. Hayashi is said to be of a silent disposition

abstain from politics. It is therefore hcld that he is

Fobryary 9, 1934.
Nakajima was violently attacked, and
his resignation demanded, in the Diet for
having published several years ago a piece
of writing in which Ashikaga Taka-uji
(a l4th century figure whose memory is
execrated as the only man in Japanese history
to have attempted to seize the throne) was
referred to in not uncomplimentary terms.
However, it would seem from Tokyo's telegram
No. 24 of February 9, 4 p.m. that there were
practical reasons for the attack on Nakajima.
The dignity of the Imperial Family is
only too frequently used to cloak political
manoeuvres. In this particular case.
Nakajima's views on Taka-uji seemed to cause
no concern for several years, the hue and
ory being raised when it became politically
expedient for someone to see Nakajima
removed from office.
I cannot identify Toji Matsumoto.

Baron Nakajima, Ninister of Commerce and Industry,
has resigned from the Cabinet and has been aucceeded by
Mr. Toji Matsumoto. It is not anticipated that this
change will affect the Cabinet as a whole or that it
indicates any change in policy. The resignation may
be attributed to dissatisfaction with Nakajima's hand-
ling of the consolidation of government and private
steel works, it being alleged that his relations with
the latter were too close.

Ichira Hatovama, late Minister of"
CP
Education, is a son-in-law of Dr. Suzuki,
president of the Seiyukai, and one of the
leaders of the Suzuki faction which has
been resisting efforts on the part of two
insurgent factions to wrest the presidency
of the party from Dr. Suzuki. A few weeks
ago, it was charged that Hatoyama had
received a fee of Yen 50,O00 for the use of
his"good offices" towards the granting
(by the Emperor) of decorations towards
certain individuals. Hatoyama made a tacit
admission of the charge, and his resignation
from the Government was considered to be
only a matter of time.
We have received no reports indicating
what the consequences of the change in the
Government will be. The chances are that
there will be secessions from the Seiyukai,
but we are now informed whether any change
in the organization of the party will
affect the position of the Saito Cabinet.

The Secretary of State,

Sir:
228
In confirmation of my telograms Nos. 66/and 68 229
of April 12, 11 A.M. and April 16, 10 A.M. respectively,
all
I have the honor to report that General Hayashi, Minister
of War, tendered his resignation to Premier Saito on
'April 1l. Four days later, on April 15, he withdrew his
resignation and consented to remain as Minister of War.
The ineident, now passed, appears to have no es-
pecial significance beyond that revealed by the simple
Î facts. As the Department is aware, General Hayashi's
brother,

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
brother, Tukichi Shiragami, was under indietment in
connection with the Tokyo Gas Company seandal of 1929
and on April 1l Shiragami was sentenced to ten months
imprisonment for bribery. Immediately upon passage of
the sentence the Minister of War tendered his resigna-
tion on personal grounds and retired to nis privato
home, this being in keeping with the usual course of
action prescribed in Japan for public officials whose
family honor has been stained.
During the days following Hayashi's resignation
he remained obdurate to the pleas addressed to him by
his colleagues in the Cabinet and the War Ministry and
it was finally considered necessary to send for Prince
Kanin, Chief of the General Staff, who was absent in
the country. Prince Kanin returned to Tokyo on the
ovening of April 14 and sumnoned Hayashi who explained
the reasons for his resignation and remained firm in
his intention to refuse to reconsider his action. On
the following day the Prince summoned General Hayashi.
and General Mazaki, Inspeotor General of Military Edu-
cation, and after conference Hayashi was persuaded to
withdraw his resignation and to continue in office.
This he did and on April l6 was received by the Emperor
on which occasion he submitted a full explanation for
his aotions.
The only importance of this incident is the situa-
tion in which the Saito Cabinet was placed for the brief
period in which there was a possibility that Hayashi
might really withdraw. Few Japanese cabinets have suo-
cessfully withstood the weakening influence of successive
ohane

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
changes, but the Saito Cabinet is proving the exception;
nevertheless had Hayashi aotually withdrawn it might
have served as the last straw in this case and it was
with this in mind that those members of the Army who
are not enthusiastically in favor of the present régime
indulged in intrigue in an attempt to prevent the War
Minister from reconsidering his action in the hopes
that his resignation might prove the end of the Saito
Cabinet.
Hayashi's great popularity, however, with the
majority of the people, as well as with his colleagues
and the Throne,served far to outweigh the influence of
intrigue and the Cabinet undoubtedly emerges somewhat
stronger than it was before Hayashi's resignation.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

IXI. INTENNAL POLIYICAL AFFAIRS:
(a) V    
The resigmation of the Minister of War, Semeral
Hayashi, by way of accepting responsibility for his brother's
complicity in the Tokyo Municipal scandal, gave the cabinet
a severe fright and the nation a shock. Eventually he
was persuaded that his duty to the nation dumanded his
retention of ofrice, by Prince Kanin who as Chief of the
General Staff and as a member of the Imperial Family
is considered to have voiced the desire of the Emperor.
Whether or not the Cabinet itself would have fallon
had General Heyashi insisted on resigmation remains uncertaim.
But the Cebinet undoubtedly is shaky, and a strong faction
of the Army is willing to overthrow the present government
with the end in view of lacing General Araki or his liou-
tenants in power. Moreover, the support of the political
parties is half-hearted, the Seiyukai in partioular
cherishing ambitions of a return to power which its large
majority in the Diet would entitle it to under more ordinary
conditioms.
A problem yet unsolved, which continues to coneerm
the C&binet is that of finding & satisfactory Minister of
dueatiom to fill the post vacated by Mr. Hatoyama who
resigmed during the Diet sessiom. Premier Saito, whe
eomtinues to hold the portfolio, has attempted to find a
smesesser to Hatoyame, a member of the Seiyakai, in the
ramks of the same party, thereky hepins to maintain the
"Balamee

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
"balanee of Pewer in the Cabanet. Thus far he has been um-
suceessfal, possibly beeause of the disimelinatiom of in-
dividual members to board a sinking ship.
Unfortunately the Cabinet is not popular in any quarter
except with the liberals, and does not command the public
supnort as it has a right to expeet. Criticism there is in
plenty, but evidence is lacking of that whole-hearted co-
operation which a national Cabinet in a period of "national
crisis" demands. The major difficulties facing Premier
Baite remaim unsolved. To the extent that the nation has
recovered from the shoek of the Mey 15th outrages, the
missiom with which the Premier was originally charged has
been fulfilled, but the causes which gave birth to that
movement are still existent. The Seiyukai, the Minseito
and the Kokumin Domei are all confident that they can save
the country, while the fascist elements which would do away
with the Diet regard themselves as equally potential saviors.
But so far no party or person has come forward to suggest
a workable altermative to the pr sent Cabinet's painful
groping. Possibly none cam be found. (See also despatch

VISICH OF
The New Okada Cabinet and Japan's Navai Program -
The Saito Cabinet's fall on July 3, 1934, was not unexpected.
For months, the political prognosticators had been announcing its im-
minent resignation for causes which in other times would have been suf-
894.002
ficient for the purpose. When the long rumored financial scandal in-
volving Kuroda, Vice Minister of Finance, among others, was officially
announced in May, it was conceded that the cabinet's tenure was limited.
providing the charges against Kuroda were sustained.
894.002/248
The resignation of Takahashi, the Finance Minister and one of
the strongest men in the Saito Cabinet, should have satisfied the Japa-
nese sense of official responsibility for the actions of a subordinate,
and the Government would have continued its course; but several circum-
stances militated against this solution of the situation. Premier Saito
and Takahashi are both elderly men who had given their full meed of
public service before a high sense of duty impelled them to return to
office in May, 1932, following the reactionary plots which resulted in
the assassination of Premier Inukai. This same sense of service kept
them at their posts in the face of severe criticism; but it is likely
that Premier Saito refused to consider going on without the counsel and
support of his old friend, Takahashi. The cabinet, moreover, had been
woakened by five resignations since it was first organized, and by the
fact that no sui table appointee to take the vacant portfolio of the
Minister of Education had been found -- for political reasons of course.
lMost damaging of all, the irregularity involving the Vice Minister of
Finance was also charged against Baron Nakajime, former Minister of Com-
merce in the Saito Cabinet, and Chuzo Mitsuchi, Minister of Railways.
This official malfeasance was too heavy a blow for a Government which
came into being at the peak of public indignation over similar iniquities
in previous party cabinets, and which had practically pledged itself to
avoid them. There was, then, nothing for the Saito Cabinet to do but
resign, when the charges against the Vice Minister of Finance were fine
FILED
ly confirmed.
G
3
While the resignation of the Saito Cabinet was no surprise,
selection of Admiral Keisuke Okada, former Minister of the Navy under
9
Premier Saito, to head a new Government was generally unexpected. He
proved to be as much of a "dark horse" as his immediate predecessor had
been. The reappointment of Admiral Saito had been considered a likeli-
hood by many, and, while Admiral Okada's name had been casually men-
tioned in the press toward the very end of the Saito regime, the names
of General Ugald, Governor General of Korea; Baron Kiyoura, former
Premier: Baron Hiranuma, reactionary leader; Dr. Suzuki, President of
the Seiyukai Party: and General Arald, fomer Minister of War, had all
been frequent subjects of speculation for months in connection with the
Premiership. The first named had small chance of muccess because he is
22
too much of a liberal to suit the army and navy; the second, because he
1s too aged -- ho is 84; the third, because he has lost some prestige
of late; the fourth, because public distrust of the political parties
has not suffisiently abated: and the fifth, because his flamboyance,
-14848-

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
ostensibly patriotic, while Minister of War created an unfavorable im-
pression among his more conservative fellow countrymen. None of the
criticisms directed toward these candidates applied to Admiral Okada.
He is 66; has had administrative experience, first as Vice Minister of
the Navy in 1923 and 1924, then as Minister of the Navy in the Seiyukai
Government of Baron Tanaka from 1927 to 1929, and lastly in the same
post under Admiral Saito for about seven months; and represents, more-
over, an armed service whose opinions are bound to be heard durin, the
next year or two.
The procedure leading to Admiral Okada's selection was an
interesting example of the operation of the powers surrounding the Throne
in Japan. There has been much speculation as to what persons or offi-
cials would succeed to the authority of the Genro upon the death of the
aged Prince Saionji, the sole survivor of the group of elder statesmen
who began to advise the Emperor in the early days of the Meiji era. The
custom of consulting the Genro whenever a cabinet crisis occurs has been
adhered to, but Prince Saionji's extreme age -- he is 95 -- and residence
outside of the capital conceivably have prevented his keeping in close
touch with all phases of current political affairs. During recent years
it has been his habit to consult wi th one or more officials near the
Throne, especially Count Mekino, Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal; but on
this occasion he enlarged his group of consultants to include also the
Grand Chamberlain to the Emperor, the Minister of the Imperial Household.
the Chief Aide-de-Camp to the Emperor, the Lord Steward to the Emperor,
the President of the Privy Council, Premier Saito himself, and three
former Premiers. It is significant that nearly all of these men are
classed as liberals or near-liberals. It is also significant that before
Prince Saionji arrived at the conference, the other members had con-
sidered and approved Premier Saito's suggestion that Admiral Okada.
his friend, be appointed. The recommendation apparently pleased Prince
Saionji, and his presentation of it to the Emperor sealed the matter.
The whole procedure indicated that the system of seeking the advice of
so-called Elder Statesmen on behalf of the Emperor will not be allowed to
die with Prince Saionji. It indicated, too, the probable membership of
the group to succeed to his prerogatives.
Despite the pronounced liberal bias of the advisory conference,
Admiral Okada's selection was a compromise. During recent months there
hes been a noticeable trend away from the extreme policies advocated by
the army, navy, and reactionary civilian groups, but these circles are
still too powerful to be disregarded. The "Big Navy" adherents had to
be satisfied that their claims for naval equality at the scheduled 1935
Naval Conference would have adequate representation; and the appointment
of a high ranking admiral -- and a Supreme War Councillor, to boot --
seems to have quieted their fears. Admiral Okada's actions in coolly
courting unpopularity and danger by mediating between the delegates who
had accepted the London Naval Treaty and its bitterest enemy, Admiral
Kanji Kato, then Chief of the Naval General Staff, and later defending
that hated agreement in the Diet worked in his favor with the liberals.
It is possible, of course, that the naval group's knowledge of these
indiscretions on the Admiral's part was overshadowed by their realization
that he was the best Premier for their purposes that they were likely to
get under the circumstances. The fact that Admiral Okada is not a member
of any political party satisfied the reactionaries and others opposed to
parliamentary government and the politicians. The army and the indus-
-14849

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.99)
trialists have at least voiced no objections.
The chief group opposing Admiral Okada is the Seiyukai Party
itself, regardless of his service in a Seiyukai Cabinet under Baron
Tanaka, and of his efforts as mediator between the Seiyukai and the Gov-
ernment during the early days of the Saito incumbency. The Seiyukai is
the majority party in the Diet, stripped of power though the latter now
is, and they have steadily hoped and worked for the return of straight
party government with Dr. Suzuki, their President, as Premier. Failing
this, the majority of the party has refused cooperation with the new
Government. The chief reason for their opposition, it is reported, is
Admiral Okada's failure to open the way for the acquisition of the Pre-
miership during the days of his mediation. Indeed, it is said he re-
sined from the Naval Ministry actually because he was unable to carry
out his commi tments to the Seiyukai and not merely because of ill health
upon reaching naval retirement age, as was stated at the time. The
Minseito, the other principal party, and the more liberal of the two,
is supporting the new Government, partly, no doubt, because it too hopes
for a return to power.
The composition of the new Government ensures a regime un-
changed in essentials from that of Admiral Saito. Hirota, General Haya-
shi, and Admiral Osumi have been retained in the Ministries of Foreign
Affairs, Army, and Navy, respectively. Fumio Goto, Minister of Agricul-
ture in the last cabinet, has been shifted to the important post of
Minister of Home Affairs. Seven of the eleven Ministers (Admiral Okada
has temporarily assumed the portfolio of Overseas Affairs) including the
Premier are nonparty men, although one of them, Goto, is believed to have
Minseito leanings. There are two regular Minseito appointees and three
from a small dissident wing of the recalcitrant Seiyukai. Thus is con-
trived another "national" cabinet similar to Admiral Saito's and, in
reality, the only kind that has any chance of surviving in present day
Japan, even though the pendulum of public opinion is beginning to swing
back from the extremely nationalistic and antiparliamentary attitude of
a year or two ago. It can be justly considered a credit to the political
perspicacity of those powers before (not behind) the Throne in Japan.
The fact that the Government faces the probability of an upset at the
hands of the Seiyukai when the Diet meets next Jamuary is a problem that
can be handled when the time comes.
The assurance of a continuation of the Saito policies comes
mainly from the retention of the incumbents in the three most important
posts of the Government aside from the Premiership, namely. Foreign Af-
fairs. the Army. and the Navy. Hirota, a man of unusual detemination
and personality, has already made some headway in the difficult task of
solving Japan's international problems through peaceful means, despite
the interference and pressure constantly exerted upon the Foreign Office
by the armed services. General Hayashi, who last January succeeded the
argressive General Arakd at the War Office, is a man of fewer words than
the latter and seems to be inclined to direct the army's activities to
matters of national defense rather than to political questions. The
following excerpt from a statement made by him last April is illuminative
of his personal concept of his office.
"I will lend my full cooperation to Mr. Hirota, the
Foreign Minister, in his efforts to further friendly
-14850

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.99)
relations with other nations. It is far from my intention
to enter into any quarrel with other countries. It is of
great importance for Japan to establish the friendliest
relations with all her neighbors and with all nations.
Any policy of isolation or of extremism following as a
result of the spirit of 'desperation' should be avoided
in our foreign relations at all costs."
Regardless of General Heyashi's personal predilections, howover, he is
subject to pressure from the younger officers in the army who have been
responsible for numerous incidents since the Kwantung Army seized lukden,
Manchuria, in September, 193l, and from the Kwantung Army itself which
does not always see eye to eye with the Government at Tokyo on rnatters
affecting Japanese policies on the northeastern Asiatic mainland. The
Kwantung Army's scheme (unsuccessful up to now) to reorganize the South
Manchuria Railway and its many allied economic enterprises as a part of
that army's ovm Special Service Section is an example of General Haya-
shi's problems from one direction.
Admiral Osumi, the Navy Minister, has never been a conspicu-
ous advocate of naval equality for Japan of the same caliber as Adrirel
Kanji Kato, Admiral Suetsugu. Commander in Chief of the Combined Fleet,
and Admiral Isoruku Yamamoto, who will be the ranking Japanese naval ex-
pert at the scheduled naval parley; but being a loyal navy man, he
wants, naturally, as big a navy for Japan as can be had. He, as well as
Admiral Okada, is knowm as a "political admiral" who has had more ad-
ministrative experience than sea duty and has not taken sides on the
London Naval Treaty. This latter fact makes Admiral Osumi acceptable
to the aggressive younger officers in the navy and enables him to act as
mediator between the two groups into which Japanese admirals appear to
be divided, i.e., those composed of uncompromising foes of the navel
limitation treaties, and those who have some realization of the financial
and international issues involved in the rejection of limitation. The
Prenier and the Navy Minister are believed to belong to the latter sroup,
despite their recent emphatic utterances on the subject of naval parity
for Japan, as reported in the press. The difficulties of their position
are exemplified in the recent meeting of 15 of the highest ranling naval
officers under the presidency of the Chief of the Naval General Staff,
H.I.H. Prince uahimi. No communique was issued afterward, but the pur-
pose of the meeting could only have been to impress the country with
the navy's detemination to carry out its program. On July 1, the press
reported that some 60 Japanese naval officers with rank of captain or
higher took the unprecedented step of submitting a petition to the Throne
through official channels, urging abrogation of the naval treaties and
the formation of a strong cabinet. The petition purported to represent
all naval ranks. Since the Chief of the Naval General Staff non has
direct access to the Throne, the petition may very well have gone to the
Emperor over the heads of the Navy Minister and the Premier.
Japan's lack of a concrete program during the recent prelimi-
nary naval disarmament conversations in London, which was the primary
cause of the adjournment of the talks until October, was probably due
to the struggle now going on between the moderates and the uxtrerifsts
in Japan as well as to the uncertain status of the Saito Government.
WIthin the week following the completion of his Cabinet on July 8, Pre-
mier Okada called a conference of the so-called "Big Five" Ministers
-14851-

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.99)
including himself and the heads of the Foreign, War, Navy, and Finance
Offices for the purpose of formulating Japan's diplomatic and military
policies, with particular reference to the coming naval parleys. Ac-
cording to the press, Admiral Osumi declared at the conference thet the
navy was determined to claim parity, abrogation or revision of the
5-5-3 naval ratio, and the perfection of Japan's sea defenses against
external aggression. On July 20, the Government issued a vague general
statement of its foreign and domestic policies which indicated a course
little different from that of the Saito Cabinet. The statement expressed
support of the peaceful settlement of international disputes, backed by
adequate armaments, which is the principle adopted by Premier Saito's
five-Minister conference last October.
The most interesting development in Japan's campaign for naval
equality since the new Government came into power was Premier Okade's
interview with the forein presss on July 3l. The following questions
submitted by the correspondents at that time and the Premier's answers
throuh an interpreter, as reported both by the Associated Press and the
New York Herald-Tribune representatives, are illuminating. Only the
most pertinent for the purposes of this article have been selected.
Q. "What are the prospects for the naval conference?"
A. "To reduce the burden of taxation weighing down
upon the peoples of the world, naval armaments must be
reduced to the lowest possible extent. Naval limita-
tion must come by the reduction of armaments by the
most powerfully armed nations."
Q. "Will Japan ask for naval parity or reduction
by the most powerfully armed nations down to the
Japanese level?" A. "It is not in my mind to ex-
pect such a radical change so suddenly, but I can-
not favor the present ratio principle, which hurts
the self-respect of nations."
Q. "Does Japan intend to abrogate the Washington
Treaty?" A. "Whether it is better or not to abro-
gate the Washington Treaty is a matter under con-
sideration and not yet settled."
When asked to name a better system of naval limitation than the ratio
principle, the Premier said he hoped a better method would be discovered
but he was not in a position to make a statement on the subject.
Premier Okada's reference to the need for lifting the burden
of taxation from the world's peoples recalls his own action in l928,
when he was Minister of the Navy, in offering to cut the navy's funds to
any extent necessary for the nation's welfare -- this in sharp contrast
to other Ministers who were urging higher appropriations for their own
departments. The next remark quoted has been interpreted in the Ameri-
can press as indicating the Japanese do not necessarily mean "parity"
when they refer to "equality." which might be termed a distinction with-
out a difference. The Washington (or Five Power) Naval Treaty will ex-
pire on December 31, l936, provided one of the signatories gives notice
two years beforehand of intention to terminate it; otherwise it expires
two years from the date of any subsequent abrogation. The London Haval
-14852

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
Treaty of 1930 also expires on December 31, 1936, except for certain
provisions relating to aircraft carriers which extend throughout the
life of the l922 pact. The Foreign Office has thus far forestalled the
desire of naal extremists to give notice of abrogation before the re-
quired time. It is understood Hirota particularly wishes to avoid any
such action prfor to the London conversetions in October.
The whole interview breathed caution, undoubtedly evoked as
much by the necessities of the internal situation as by the desire to
protect Japan's position abroad. According to the press, however, it
met with the approval of naval circles.
In summing up the internal situation in Japan with reference
to the naval program, two opposing trends of opinion are discernible.
On the one hand, is a cabinet of moderate tendencies committed to a poli-
cy of peaceful settlement of international disputes. Two of the key men
in this Government, the Premier and the Navy Minister, are noted for
their ability to balance antagonistic elements, to mediate in difficult
isgues. Admiral Osumi, in particular, is characterized as clever, tact-
ful, obliging. intelligent, communicative -- qualities which will serve
him well in the future. A third key man is Fujii, the new Finance
Minister, who served as Vice Minister under his predecessor, Tatahashi,
and presumably is thoroughly in sympathy with the latter's policies of
moderate inflation and reduction of military expenditures. Renged be-
hind the cabinet are some of the most powerful men of the realm, namely,
the close advisers and associates of the Emperor who determined the com-
plexion of the present regime. These men are internationalists, liberals,
and supporters of a parliamentary government. Even before the cabinet
crisis they showed their temper by appointing the liberal Baron Dr. Ikki
as President of the Privy Council over the head of the incumbent Vice
President, Baron Hiranuma, fascist leader already mentioned, who ordi-
narily would have succeeded to the position. The moderates also have
the support of some of the most powerful industrialists.
On. the ether side, are found the naval extremists who stand
for naval parity and, failing that, would cast aside all limitation, re-
gardless of results. They are supported by part of the navy, most of
the army including the redoubtable General Arakd, all of the reactionary
societies, the great Mitsui financial house, part of the Seiyukai Party,
and a number of the biggest newspapers. These extremists in the navy
and the reactionaries or fascists undoubtedly would overthrow parlia-
mentarism entirely to gain their purposes; and it is their propaganda
which has created fear among the people of warlike intentions on the
part of the United States.
With this summation of the elements struggling for supremacy
in Japan, Hirota's trial balloons, such as a nonaggression pact with
the United States and the division of the Pacific between the latter and
Japan, are more understandable. The fact that the moderates are in the
ascendancy for the nonce lends a gleam of hope to an otherwise shadowed
international situation.

AHIMENTUESIE
Tokyo's No. 943, August 24, 1934:
The Embassy describes in considerable
detail the question which has been agitating
the Japanese during the entire summer --
centralising Japanese control and administra-
tion of "Manchukuo". It will be recalled that
there are now three principal Japanese
organizations in Manchuria, all theoretically
indepondent of each other: The Embassy at
Hsinking, the Kwantung Army, and the Govern-
ment of Kwantung, General Hishikari being
Commander of the Kwantung Army as well as
Ambassador.
With a view to unifying control, it was
proposed by the Army that the Ambassador
assume control over the Kwantung Government,
so that he would have the powers of a viceroy,
and that he should be responsible to the
Japanese Prime Minister in regard to all the
matters under his governance -- administrative,
diplomatic and military -- rather than to
the Foreign Minister in regard to diplomatic
matters and to the War Office in regard to
military matters.
This

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
This proposal has been ardently opposed
by the Foreign Office for the obvious reason
that it would be incompatible with Japan's
contention that "Manchukuo" is an independent
'istate.
Press despatohes from Tokyo report that
compromise has been reached: the Ambassador
will continue to be responsible to the
Minister for Foreign Affairs in regard to
diplomatic matters and, in his other capacity
as Commander of the Kwantung Army, he will
report to the War Office on military matters.
The Office of Governor of Kwantung will be
abolished, and the duties of that office will
be carried out by a "Secretary-General to
the Ambassador", who will have charge of the
administration of the Kwantung Leased
Territory and the Railway Zone. The press
reports that a promise has been given to the
Army to give consideration to the appointment
of an army officer as "Secretary-General to
the Ambassador".
I assume that the Embassy will submit a
supplementary report, describing the arrange-
ment finally agreed upon.
The subject is an important one, as re-
vealing the pressure being exerted by the
Japanese Army for Japan to move towards open
admission of the true status of "Manchukuo".

for an Overseas Ministry. It was subsequently reported,
however, that the Cabinet would not abolish that Ministry,
indicating that the purely colonial matters in Manchuria

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)

diplomatic, administrative and military missions,
and advocate a supreme Japanese Administration to be
located at Hsingking. That body would anpoint an adminis-
trator who would have diplomatic, military and civil
powers, would supervise the South Manchuria Railway and
direct the important policies connected with economic
development of "Manchukuo". Civil government in the
Leased Territory would be continued under the Kwantung
officials' plan. The activities of the Foreign Office
would then be limited to diplomatic form: lities.
From indications in the several press articles
and news agenciest reports it anpears that the Foreign
Ministerwill maintain his stand that in order not to
undo all of his work in promoting recognition of "Man-
chukuo" the representative to that country must be
diplomatic, whether or not he may also exercise adminis-
trative functions. The War Office seems equally firm
in maintaining that the Premier and to a degree the Army
should have control over the administrative matters,
which do not concern the Foreign Office, and since it is
agreed to allow the Officer in command of the Kwantung
garrison

=== Page 351 === (confidence 1.00)

garrison to combine the function of Ambassador (the Foreign
Office observes that that was to be a temporary measure)
he should be the supreme representative, and should not

The altercation between the three Ministries is of
great interest in that the outcome will be a good in-
dication of the future policy of the Japanese Government;
shall the Military continue to exercise the dominant in-
fluence in external policies, or shall the Foreign Office
consolidate its position vhich has been gaining the as-
cendency of late, and control the anchurien representa-
tion question?
