DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 14, 1934.
Tokyo's despatch No. 1005, dated
October 10, 1934:
The Embassy describes the agreement
reached by the Cabinet over the reform of
Japanese administration in "Manchukuo" --
a problem which has aroused considerable
agitation among the public and acrimony between
the Government departments. The plan, as
adopted, seems to be a signal victory for
the Army, and a defeat for the Foreign and
Overseas Ministries. It has yet to receive
the approval of the Frivy Council and Diet,
and may be further amended.
Hithertofore there have been three
principal Japanese administrative organiza-
tions in Manchuria, all theoretically in-
dependent of each other: the Embassy at
Hsinking, the Government of Kwantung, and
the Kwantung Army, General Hishikari being
Commander of the Kwantung Army as well as
Ambassador.
The new plan is substantially as
follows: The triple system of control shall
be changed to a dual system, whereby all
authority

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
authority shall be exercised through the
Ambassador and Commander-in-Chief of the
Kwantung Army. However, these two posts
will be held by one man, who will be an
Army officer. He will be responsible
directly to the Premier, although respon-
sible in a limited degree to the Foreign
Minister, and will have supervision over
all Japanese interests in Manchuria, includ-
ing the Kwantung Government and the S.M.R.
In Tokyo a new Manchurian Affairs
Bureau will be established as a Cabinet
bureau directly responsible to the Premier.
As the chief of this Bureau is to be of
"Shinnin" rank, it amounts virtually to
the creation of a new Cabinet portfolio.
Incidentally, an Army man is to head this
Bureau.
The decision is interesting and
important. It demonstrates that the
Military are still absolute in matters of
policy; that the Army is determined to brook
no interference from the civilian government
in Manchurian matters; and most important,
that it reveals the pressure of the Army
toward making an actual dependency of the
"Independent Empire of Manchukuo".
2.

Jonorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
894.002/251
No. 943 of August 24, 1934, regarding the disagreement
in the Japanese Cabinet over the change in representatio
EC131934
FILED
and administration in Manchuria, and to report that an
agreement has finally been reached between the several
Cabinet members concerned. The final accord, decided by
unanimous ballot, was immediately acclaimed by the press
as a signal victory for the Army, as the new plan embodies
most of the demands of the War Ministry and sacrifices
221

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
all but a few of those suggested by the Foreim and
Overseas Ministries. However, although the Cabinet
has agreed on the plan, which Premier Okada presented
as embodying certain concessions on all sides, the con-
troversy does not appear to be settled. Considerable
adverse comment has been heard, and many editorials
have appeared voicing as strong opinions as they dare
that aimiristration of Manc uria by the Army will not
prove successful. Moreover, the Foreign and Overseas
Ministries have not given up the fight although their
Ministers have agreed to Army control of Japanese organs
in Manchuria. The very existence of the Overseas Minis-
try is threatened, in spite of the Premier's repeated
denial that the Government intends to abolish that port-
folio. In Kwantung, the officials of the civil Govern-
ment are still protesting volubly against the proposed
plan, and some six hundred of them including high offi-
cials presented their resignations. They were prevailed
upon to reconsider, but are still very dissatisfied and
may carry out their intention to resign later. The civil
police of the Kwantung administration, who will now be
placed under Army control instead of the Overseas Ministry,
have made more serious threats. Bloodshed has been threa-
tened, according to the press, if the Cabinet insists on
enforcing that part of the new plan which provides that
the Chief of the Military Gendarmerie will also head the
civil police in the Kwantung Leased Territory. Although
police

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
police resignations have been refused once, there is
serious agitation, according to the JAPAN TIMES on the
part of police officials to insist on their resignations
being accepted. The Premier, in his capacity as Overseas
Minister, has been appealed to so strongly that he has
promised to review the whole plan in an effort to provice
against complete absorption of the civil by the military
officials in the Kwantung and South Manchuria Railway
districts.*
Newspaper editorials term the reform of administra-
tion in Manc uria one of Premier Okada's knottiest problems.
All during the difficult negotiations, which were character-
ized by the Army's obdurate st nd, the press observed that
probabilities of arriving at an agreement were daily be-
coming less promising. The Army officials refused to
yield an inch, believing that inasmuch as they had "fought
and bled to save Manchuria", and that civil administration
was equivalent to control by corrupt political parties,
they were the ones indicated to guide the future develop-
ment of the new country, and that only they understood the
situation there. The Foreign Office, of course, reiterated
its stand that if "Manchukuo" were to be considered an in-
dependent country the Japanese representative must be un-
der its control, and not an official comparable to a Colonial
Governor. The Overseas Office, seeing all of its control
over the administrative organs in Manchuria slipping away,
voiced loud protests against the Army attempting to assume
administrative functions for which there was no precedent.
The effectiveness of its protests, was however, greatly
minimized because there is no Overseas Minister as yet to
fight
*Bee Despatch to this Embassy from Dairen dated Beptember
21, 1934, on Reorganization Plan.

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
fight its battles; the portfolio being held concur-
rently by the Premier.
Inasmuch as the situation was becoming increasingly
more delicate, a solution had to be found, if only of
a temporary nuture. Pressure from all sides was brought
to bear. The supreme Military Council discussed the
various reform plans, the Genro's advice was sought,
technical experts were summoned to contrive a workable
compromise, and Vice Ministers and Section Chiefs met
repeatedly in an effort to evolve a plan for reorganizing
the administrative control in Manchuria as well as to
determine what sort of representative Japan should have
in the new "Empire of Manchukuo". To avoid further
friction the Cabinet refrained from meeting over the
problem until the members were ready to come to some de-
cision. Finally Premier Okada gathered all the reins
in his own hands, called the Cabinet together and pre-
sented it with a compromise plan which embodied vir-
tually all of the Army's demands. The plan was adopted
by unaimous vote, and the Cabinet had accomplished some-
thing. At least some of the newspapers congratulated
the Premier for having brought about an agreement at all,
even if it is only temporary.
The plan has now to receive the approval of the Privy
Council and then be presented before the extraordinary
session of the Diet for its approval. It is reported
that the Diet session may take place during the last
half of November, and another sharp struggle is antici-
pated. Foreign Office and especially Overseas Ministry
supporters

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
supporters in the Diet are expected to join with the
anti-military bloc in attempting to modify the reform
plan. But the opinion of military sympathizers seems
to be that the Army will again succeed in forcing most
of its demands through. It is eager to put the new
plan into operation to avoid further dissension in
Manchuria, and urges that the original plan of putting
the reform into effect next spring be advanced to Novem-
ber or December of this year.
Premier Okaca's compromise plan, as reported by
the ASAHI, the JAPAN TIMES and the ADVERTISER -- all
three reports vary somewhat -- is substantially as follows:
1. The present triple system of control through the
Ambassador, the Commander-in-Chief of the Kwantung Army
and the Governor General of Kwantung Territory shall be
abolished, and instead a dual system of control through
the Ambassador and the Commander-in-Chief of the Kwan-
tung Army shall be established.
2. The Ambassadorial post and that of the Commander-
in-Chief of the Kwantung Army will be held by one man,
who will, it is understood, be a high ranking Army Of-
ficer. He will be under the direct control of the Pre-
mier although responsible in a limited degree to the
Foreign Minister for activities purely diplomatic. He
will be vested with supervisory and administrative
rights, such as supervision of the South Manchuria Rail-
way Company and the Manchuria Telegraph and Telephone
Company; he will also control the Governor of the Kwan-
tung Province and administration of the South Manchuria
Ra11ay

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Railway Zone. (The foregoing was reluctantly agreed
to by the Foreign and Overseas Offices upon being
persuaded that it is not a permanent arrangement, but
necessary because Manchuria is still in the throes of
transition, and military guidance is wiser for a time.)
3. A Chief Administrative Officer of "Chokunin"
rank (Cabinet appointee) shall be named to supervise
the administrative affairs. He will act as a Counselor
under the Ambassador, in addition to the regular diplo-
matic Counselor. It is understood that he will be an
Army Officer of Major-General rank appointed by the
Premier. He will control the three departments of General
Affairs, Police and Superintendence. Under him the
Chief of the General Affairs Department will take charge
of general administrative business such as engineering,
education and sanitation; the Chief of the Police Depart-
ment will attend to administrative and police affairs
in the South Manchuria Railway Zone, and other special
functions; the Chief of the Superintendence Department
shall supervise the business of the South Manchuria Rail-
way Company and the Telegraph and Telephone Company, and
shall also hold the position of Chief of Military Traffic.
The provision that the office of Chief of Police
shall be held concurrently by the Commandant of the Kwan-
tung Gendarmerie -- a Major-General -- has caused all
the trouble among the civil police in the Kwantung Pro-
vince. Thus far, at an indignation meeting of police,
a Chief Inspector was killed by one of his subordinates
who accused him of cowardice in demanding a revision of
the

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7-
the proviso from the Japanese Government. As feeling
is running high, further trouble may be expected if
a compromise plan is not effected. The ADVERTISER
quotes the HOCHI as expressing great surprise at the
mystery of why the civil officials in Kwantung Province
are so belligerently iissatisfied with the change in
administration in Manchuria. The implication seems to
be that they may have been prompted by important in-
fluences at home.
4. The present Governorship of the Kwantung Leased
Territory will be abolished, and a new Governor of "Cho-
kunin" rank will be appointed to take charge of adminis-
trative business in the Province, though not in the Rail-
way Zone, under the direction of the Chief Administrative
Officer. He will not have the powers of a Provincial
Governor. The courts, communications and Technical School
at Port Arthur shall be controlled directly by the Am-
bassador.
5. There shall be no revision of the regular diplo-
matic functions of the Embassy. The regular Counselor
shall direct the consular business, and the judicial and
registration affairs, besides the judicial police business
in the railway zone.
1
6. At the same time, in Tokyo, there will be establish-
ed under the direct supervision of the Premier, a new Man-
churian Affairs Bureau as part of the Cabinet. The Presi-
dent of that Bureau will be a person of "Shinnin" rank
(Imperial appointee). The Bureau will be composed of an
y advisory council made up of the Directors of Departments
concerned

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
1 concerned, and will communicate the Premier's instruc-
tions to the Ambassador in "Manchukuo" and provide a
liaison between the Japanese Government and the Japan-
ese administrative and representative organs in Manchu-
ria. Some of the Departments now under the Ministry
of Overseas Affairs will be transferred to this new
Bureau, and the Overseas Minister will transmit his
instructions to Consular Officers and others having
to do with colonization work in Manchuria, through the
Foreign Minister. This Bureau was not included in the
Army's original plan.
The ADVERTISER of September 19 points out that the
selection of the head of the new Bureau is one of the
most important steps in the entire reform plan. As he
is to be of "Shinninkan" rank, it amounts virtually to
the creation of a new Cabinet portfolio. The Govern-
ment's plan contemplated placing a high ranking General
on the active list at the head of the Bureau and a civilian
as Vice-President. The Army insisted that the plan be re-
versed in order that an Army man be in active control of
the organs in Manchuria as Vice-President, but that the
Bureau not have the appearance of being purely military,
a civilian be in charge. However, the Premier finally
prevailed upon the War Minister to adopt his plan, and
General Hayashi has agreed to hold the Presidency himself
: in addition to his portfolio as War Minister. The Govern-
ment hopes, by having a civilian Vice-President adminis-
trating its orders to the organs in Manchuria, that the
disgruntled civilian officers there will be appeased.
The

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
The victory of the Army in the Manchurian reform
plan, says the ADVERTISER of September 19, is a dupli-
cation of the victory won by the Navy in the question
of abrogation of the Washington Treaty. The NICHI NICHI
in July wanted to know what position the Army and Navy
would hol in the Okada Cabinet. The press now seems
to be of the opinion, though not expressed in so many
words, that the military dominates the Government in
spite of the efforts of the anti-military bloc, and the
two victories aforementioned appear to bear out that
belief. One paper pointed out, in connection with the
Manchurian reform, that the public had much more confi-
dence in the military than in civil administration, and
that was why the Army could feel confident that its de-
mands would be granted. However, the fact that the strug-
gle was so long drawn shows that the anti-military element
has some strength. It was suggested that the hand of
General Araki was shown in the belligerent attitude as-
sumed by the Kwantung Army toward the reorganization
plan, and other incidents indicate that the Army fears
the growth of anti-military opinion among the people.
Mr. Hirota, the Foreign Minister, did not lose entirely
as the Army does not have complete control of the organs
in Manchuria. The Premier himself is to direct the new
Bureau in the Cabinet. At the same time, although agree-
ment has been reached over the Manchurian administration
it remains to be seen whether or not the plan will be
modified

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.95)
-10-
modified by the Privy Council or by the Diet. The
tremendous budgetary demands made by the Army and Navy
for the ensuing year may have great influence in re-
ducing their popularity with the public and domination
in the Government.
Respectfully yours,

Tokyo's despatch No. l019, dated
October 20, 1934:
This despatch, describing the agitation
against the reform plan for administration
in Manchuria, should be read in conjunction
with the Embassy's No. 1005 of October 10,
1934, which described the proposed changes.
The Japanese civilian police in
Manchuria have been agitating vociferously
against being placed under gendarmerie
control, probably fearing loss of prestige
and possibly of their jobs. This agitation
has served to draw attention to the larger
question: that of civil administration
versus military administration. The long-
standing controversy waged around the War
Office and Overseas Ministry, and to a
leaser degree the Foreign Office, has
apprently not been settled by the decision
by the Cabinet to adopt the plan described
in the Despatch of October l0. On the
contrary, the dispute now threatens to
affect the stability of the Cabinet. The
civilian police resistance to the new plan
is so determined that it threatens to
become

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
become a revolt against the Military. Premier
Okada. who is also Overaeas Minister, will
have to accept responsibility for this
insubordination, and may have to resign.
On the other hand, he can withdraw the
Cabinet plan for reform without loss of
face which may require resignation.
According to rumor the powers close
to the throne are dissatisfied with the
Okada Government because it has not been
able to withstand the demand7"Because of
the difficult times ahead of the nation,
careful and peaceful diplomacy is needed,
but offorts along these lines are constantly
being fustrated by the violent views of
the Military, which Okada seems powerless
to curb. The present incident, therefore,
Is being used to force the Okada Cabinet
out of office.
One newspaper states that Viscount Ishii,
Viscount Saito, and Mr. Takahashi are
mentioned as possible successors to
Premier Okada.

OF STATE
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
252.
1005/dated October 10, 1934, on the subject of Japap-
3
ALED
representation in Manchuria, and to report that, sinee
8
-1934
that despatch was written, the dispute referred to on
pages 6 and 7, between the civil police of the Kwantung
Leased Territory and the Kwantung Army Gendarmerie, has
developed to a point where it threatens the continued
Vexistence of the Okada Cabinet.
Under the plans which have been devised and approved
by the Cabinet for the reform of the Japanese administrative
orsana

=== Page 367 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
organs in Manchuria, the Commander of the Gendarmerie
of the Kwantung Army is to hold concurrently the post of
the Director of Police Affairs of the Kwantung Leased
Territory and the South Manchuria Railway Zone. As
Director of Police Affairs, the Gendarmerie Commander
will control all the Japanese civilian police in Man-
churia, including the Kwantung Government police, which
have jurisdiction in the Leased Territory and the Railway
Zone, the consular police, now under the control of the
Foreign Office, in Manchurian cities where there are
large Japanese and Korean communities, and the local
Japanese police, constables or watchmen who are hired by
the Japanese communities for their own protection. There
are around five thousand of these civilian police in Man-
churia, and for some reason which is not entirely clear,
they are objecting most strongly to being placed under
the command of the Army. The majority of them are ex-service
men, and it would naturally be thought that they would
welcome control by the Army, but it appears that they feel
that they have graduated from the Army and object to re-
turning to its control. There is also the fear that the
Gendarmerie will entirely supplant the police in Manchuria
and that therefore the members of the police force will
lose their positions..
In addition to these more or less personal reasons,
however, there is the question of civil administration
versus military administration, which is the basic problem
underlying the dispute now waging between the Ministry
of Overseas Affairs and the War Department. The police
dispute

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
dispute is only a part of this larger controversy, but
has attracted public attention because the police are
active and vociferous in their opposition to military
control.
In the course of their campaign against being
placed under the control of the Japanese Army, the Kwan-
tung police sent fourteen members of their organization
to Tokyo. Shortly after their arrival, they issued a
statement to the newspapers, of which the following is
the ADVERTISER translation:
" We are not resorting to the underhand
measures practiced by some participants
in this dispute. The military is pro-
jecting an autocratic administration in
Manchuria, which will not promote the wel-
fare of Japan and is prone to disturb or-
der in Manchuria. The population of Man-
churia does not enjoy military administra-
tion. To cite an example, the army has
set fire to and consumed in flame all the
villages along the road stretching for
20 miles between Shanchengchen and Tunghwa,
alleging that the villages have been har-
boring bandits. We question that that is
the way to enhance the friendly feeling of
the Manchurian people.
" If the military really believes in the
reorganization formula, why does it apply
pressure on freedom of opinion in Manchuria?
We demand fair play in this dispute, and we
mean to fight to the last man literally as
protectors of the Constitution."
Five newspapers in Tokyo, including the JAPAN ADVERTISER,
were suppressed for publishing this statement.
The controversy is being waged around the War Office
and the Ministry of Overseas Affairs, the latter depart-
ment having jurisdiction over the civilian administra-
tion of the Kwantung Leased Territory. Both of these
Departments have issued public statements explaining
their

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
their positions, creating what the Japanese newspapers
describe as "the battle of statements". The War Office
contends that the civilian police should be placed under
the command of the Kwantung Army because of the necessity
for unified control, while the Department of Overseas
Affairs bases its arguments on the ground that civilian
administration should always be separate from military af-
fairs. The newspapers have been rather non-committal in
regard to the matter, and confine their comments to de-
ploring the break-down in official discipline. The re-
actionary element, as is usually the case, supports the
Army view, but, according to information which has reached
the Embassy, a large section of the public is inclined to
support the civilian view of the case. It is being asserted
privately, according to one informant, that the Army is
becoming much too arrogant and grasping, and that it is not
working entirely for the good of Japan and Manchuria. It
has personal reasons for wishing to control everything
possible in Manchuria. Army officers, in the Japanese ser-
vice, are usually retired when comparatively young, on
small pensions, and they wish to create numerous positions
in Manchuria under their control, in order that they may be
able to step into these positions when they are retired.
The Army, however, is adamant in its demand that the
original plan be carried out and that the Kwantung police be
placed under the Gendarmerie commander. The Cabinet was
forced to agree with the Army and announced on the 18th
that there would be no deviation from the original scheme
for reform of the administrative organs in Manchuria. Im-
mediately upon receipt of the news, a number of the civilian
officials

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
officials of the Kwantung Government resigned, and the
Kwantung police is reported to be considering resignation
en masse. The Kwantung Army has reported that it is pre-
pared to take drastic action (presumably meaning the pro-
clamation of martial law in the Leased Territory) if neces-
sary.
The principal danger to the Okada Cabinet arises
from the fact that the Cabinet cannot withdraw its plan
for reforming the Japanese administrative organs in Man-
churia without such loss of face that it will be compelled
to resign. On the other hand, if the civilian police con-
tinue their resistance to military control, a situation
will be created which will amount practically to a revolt
against the military. Premier Okada, who is also Minister
for Overseas Affairs, will have to assume responsibility
for the insubordination of the officials of his Department,
and will have to resign. Admiral Okada is in a most diffi-
cult position. As Premier he must carry out the plans of
his Cabinet anc enforce the reform of the administrative
organs in Manchuria; as Minister for Overseas Affairs, how-
ever, he must present and uphold the principles for which
the officials of his department are battling. General
aeashe
Hishikari, the Commander of the Kwantung Army, Ambassador
to "Manchukuo", and Governor of the Kwantung Leased Terri-
tory, also is in an anomalous position. As a representa-
tive of the Army, he must support unification of control
of Japanese administration in Manchuria under the Army;
as a representative of the Foreign Office and the Overseas
Ministry he must advocate civilian administration. General
Hishikari is reported in the newspapers to have given up the
situation as beyond his control, and to be preparing to
r0s10

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
1 resim.
The rumor is current in political circles in Japan
that there is more in the present controversy than meets
the casual eye. According to the rumor, the Genro, Prince
Saionji, the Keeper of the Privy Seal, Count Makino, the
Minister of the Imperial Household, Baron Dr. Ikki, and
other officials close to the Throne, are dissatisfied with
the Okada Government, principally because it has not been
able to withstand the demands of the military. The Genro
and other pacific influences wish to save the coming naval
disarmament conference, if possible, but the Okada Cabinet
was prevailed upon by the radical elements in the Navy to con-
sent to Japan's abrogation of the Washington naval agree-
ment. Likewise, the liberal element was opposed to the
Army's plan to unify all Japanese administrative organs in
Manchuria under the control of the Army, but the Okada Cabinet
was forced or induced to agree to the Army's plans with but
slight modification. Because of the difficult times ahead
of the nation, careful, peaceful and friendly diplomacy
is needed, but efforts along these lines are continually
being frustzated by the violent views of the military, and
Premier Okada has shown himself as virtually powerless in
his efforts to curb the military. The present incident,
therefore, according to the rumors, is being used to force
the Okada Cabinet out of office.
The TEITO NICHI NICHI states that the Genro and other
high officials are already secretly casting about in search
of

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.94)
-7-
of a suitable candidate for Premier. Viscount Ishii (former-
ly Foreign Minister and Ambassador to Washington), Viscount
Saito (recently Premier) and Mr. Korekiyo Takahashi (recently
Finance Minister) are mentioned as possible candidates.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Sir:
I have the honor to report that General Jiro Minami,
Minister for War at the time of the outbreak in Manchuria
in l93l, has been appointed to the combined posts of Com-
mander of the Kwantung Army and Ambassador to "Manchukuo"
with control of the Government of the Kwantung Leased
Territory, succeeding General Takashi Hishikari, who will,
it is stated, be made a member of the Supreme War Council.
The change in personnel is stated not to involve
any change in the policy of Japan in Manchuria. Apparently
it

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.95)
-2 -
it is partly routine, and partly a desire to have a
new man in charge to carry out fully the recent changes
which the Government of Japan recently introduced in
the Japanese Governmental organs in that region. This
8940027253
subject was discussed in my despatch No. 10l9 of Octo-
ber 20, 1934.
Respectfully yours,
2く.

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
(b)
The Cabinet.
With further reference to the proposed commission
for the study of national policies which would act as an
advisory body to the Cabinet,** it was reported by the
January newspapers that, according to the Government's
plan, this commission would be composed of representatives
of the three leading political parties (the Seiyukai, the
Minseito, and the Kokumin Domei), the Army, the Navy, and
diplomatic, financial, and educational circles, and that
among those considered as possible candidates were former
Premier Makoto Saito, Baron Tatsuo Yamamoto, Finance
Minister Takahashi, Viscount Kikujiro Ishii (former
Foreign Minister!, and Baron Goh (President of the Tokyo
Chamber of Commerce and Industry). The Seiyukai are
apparently
Embassy's despatch No. 1088, December 10, 1934.
** Embassy's daspatch No. 1154, February 7, 1935.
*** See Monthly Report for October, 1934, Section III (a).

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.97)
-二”
apparently opposed to the formation cr such & commission
inasmuch as, according to press reports, they fear that
the commission will be organized as the nucleus of a new
party to support the Governnert in the event that the
Diet is dissolved end a general election is called.
Opinion is divided concerning the Cabinet's reasons for
espousing the esteblishment of an adviscry commission on
policy, some observers believing that this organization
is designed as a bulwark against the Seiyukai, others
that its purpose is to foster public belief in the
notion of united national support of the present Govern-
ment through diversified representation in the commission.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a)
The Cabinct.
(1)
Speeches of Cabinet Members at the Conference
002
of Prefectural Governors.
894.
At the opening of the first conference of Pre-
fectural Governors under the present Government on
May 3, addresses were delivered by the Fromier and by
the Ministers for Foreign Affairs, Finance, and Justice,
setting forth the administrative policies of the Govern-
ment.
The Prenier's speech was for the most part routine
in nature. Howev r, he tacitly attacked the Minobe
theory when hc stated that scholars must refrain from
utterances likely to cause an erroneous conception of
the fundamental principle of the nation, namely, faithful
obedience of all Jepenese subjects to the E peror as
ruler.
. Hs also touched on the functions of the recently
organized Cabinet Delioerative Body and Cabinet Iuvesti-
gation Bureau. Mr. Hirota said that J n was on
friendly torns with the rest of the world, including
"Manchukuo", Soviet Russia, end China; that Japan's
relations with Chia showed a "trond toward a notable
improvement at present as compared with those that
obtained in May last when the Governors' conference was
in session"; and, with reference to the unrest prevailing
in Europe since the declaration by the German Government
on March 16 of the adoption of a system of general
military conscription, that the Japenese Government was
taking a calm attitude regarding the affair, which was
a problem mainly concerning Europe. The Minister
for Justice denounced "undesirable elements" among the
extreme Rightists who oppross public opinion and
intimidate the press under the cloak of patriotism
and nationalism. Mr. Tckahashi's speech contained
nothing of interest.
(2)

- 19 -
(2)
Creation of the Cabinet Deliberative Body
and the Cabinet Investigation Bureau.
On Me: 10 the much discussed Cabinet Peliberativo
Body and also the Cabinet Investigation Bireau were
created by I  ial Ordic nces. T e Frer ier is ux-
officio Fresident of the first-named organization.
Three days after the stablishment of these bodies,
the OFFICIAI GAZFTTF announced the appointment of the
Finance Minister, Mr. Takahashi, tc the post of Vice
President of the Crbinet Deliberative Booy and named
fifteen other men as its members:
a former Premier
(Viscount Acmiral Seito); four distinguished Pcers;
one representative each for the Mitsui interests and
the Ni: o: Yusen K isha; four Jeaders of the Mi scito
party; the leader of the Kokumin Do..si party; and three
former Seiyukai leadors who had lost their party member-
ship because of their cooperaticn with the present Govern-
ment against Seiyukai orders. The Imperial Ordinance
provides that the Cabinet Deliberative Body shall be
ander the direction of the Cebinet and shall investigate
and deliberate on such important policies as may be
referred to it by the Cabinet. The Iperial Ordinance
establishing the Cebinet Investigation Bureau provides
that this organization shall investigate important
policies, shall study proposed governmental actions
as directed by the Premier, and shall handle the general
affairs of the Cabinet Deliberative Body. Mr. Shigeru
Yoshida resigned from the position of Chiof Secretary.
of the Cabinet to accept appointment as Tirector of
the Investigation Bureau. While the Army and Navy are
represented in the latter organization, they have no
representation

III. INTERVAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a)
The Cabinet.
The Cabinet Deliberative Body and he Cabinet Investi-
gation Bureau, which had been created on May l0 by Imperial
Grdinances, held their first session on June 12 and began
work on the first problem subuitted by the Government,
the reforn of central and local finances. At a second
meeting held on June l7 national policies for the re-
organization of energency finances and the promotion of
national economic activities in generel were discussed
and Cabinet Ministers vere interpellated by members of
the Cabinet Doliberative Body. The Linister for Finance
stated that he could not predict when the present un-
balanced budget would ond. In answer to an intorpellation
concerning the problem of adjusting national finances and
national defense, the War and Navy Ministers declined to
divulge their views, the former replying that he must
first make a study of the comparative strength of the
Japanese Army and the armies of other Powers and also
give special consideration to the situation in "Manchukuo",
the latter stating that he coulā not announce his opinion
until

until the results of the naval limitation conference
should become known.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DEPARTMENT OF ATAIR
ON OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 5, 1935.
General Kawashima is being transferred
to the War Office from the command of the
military forces in Korea. He has the
reputation of being an excellent administrator.
a forceful leader of troops, and a strong
character. He belongs neither to the
Araki group nor to the Ugaki group, and it
may therefore be expected that he will
continue General Hayashi's policy of
suppressing factionalism in the Army.

THE UNDER SECRETARY
OF STATE
MAR 10 1926
DIVISION
DEPARTMENT
DIVISION OF
March 5, 193
DCR
Division of
MAR 10 1936
lug
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
NOTED
KOKI HIROTA
Department of State
Koki Hipóta has been Japanese Minister for Foreign
Affairs since September 14, 1933. He was born in Fukuoka
on the 1sland of Kyushu in February 1878. His father was a
stone dealer. After graduating in 1905 from the Tokyo
Imperial University, Faculty of Law, he entered the diplo-
matic service and served first in Peking. In l909 he was
894.002/276
transferred to London and in 1914 returned to Tokyo as a
section chief in the Commercial Bureau of the Foreign Office.
In 1918 he was made First Secretary at Washington. In 1921
he was made chief of the Second Section of the Intelligence
Bureau of the Foreign Office. In 1922 he went as Counselor
to London. In 1923 he was chief of the Bureau of European
and American Affairs in the Foreign Office, in 1926 Minister
to Holland, and in l929 a representative at the League of
Nations. In l930 he went to the Soviet Union as Ambassador.
His only service in the United States was as First Secretary
from 1919 to 1921.
MAR 11 1936
Mr. Hirota enjoys a unique position in Japanese politics
by reason of his association with a patriotic society called
the Koburyu Kai (Blgok Dragon Society). This organisation
had its origin in Fukuoka, Hirota's native Province, at the
time of the Russo-Japanese War, 1904-1905, under the leadership
of

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
of Mitsuru Toyama and Ryuhei Uohida, both of whom have simee
become conspiouous public figures in the chauvinist movement.
An aim of the society was Japanese expansion in Manchuria.
Throughout his career Hirota has maintained his membership
and still continues the habit of consulting the leaders of
the organization. For the first time a Foreign Minister who
is not a soldier has kept the confidence of the military
because of the backing of a strong patriotic society.
Hirota has often been mentioned as a probability for the
premiership, and he has played his hand carefully with that
office in view. His avoidance of connection with either of
the political parties was deliberate, and the present popular
disfavor into which the political parties have fallen confirms
the wisdom of his judgment in this matter.
The Embassy at Tokyo, as early as May 3, 1935, mentioned
the frequently suggested idea of Hirota's becoming the next
prime minister.
CC
CC/DLY

A despatch from Tokyo in THE NEW YORK TIMES this
morning reports that Mr. Hachiro Arita, until recently
894.
Japanese Ambassador to China, has been installed as Foreign
Minister in Mr. Hirota's cabinet. Mr. Arita is a career
diplomat and has had the following assignments: First
Secretary of Embassy in Washington in 192l; First Secretary
894.002/28
of Legation, Peiping, in l923; Consul General at Honolulu,
Chicago, and Ottawa; Counselor of Embassy in Berlin; Chief
of the Asiatic Bureau of the Foreign Office, l927; Minister
to Austria and Hungary, 1930; Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs, 1932; Ambassador to Belgium, 1933; Ambassador to
285
China, 1936.
Mr. Arita became prominent in 1932 when he was appointed
Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs under Mr. Yoshizawa, at a
time when there was going on a bitter foud in the Foreign

F/FG

Harbin's despatch No. 335, March 17,
1936, reports on reaction in Harbin to
the Hirota Cabinet. The press welcomes
Hirota's appointment and states that,
although the Premier does not seem to
have renounced his liberal thought and
conoiliatory policy, which he must do in
order to steer the ship of state success-
fully, he at least has promised the
military a strong and positive policy.
Japanese civilians generally approve
Hirota's appointment but they fear
that new financial policies will produce
increased military expenditures with
greater liability of war. They are
doubtful that Hirota will be able to
keep the army in cheok but they do not
believe war is likely in the near future.

=== Page 472 === (confidence 1.00)
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). Formation of the Hirota Cabinet and its
Statement of Policy.
The Okada Cabinet resigned en bloc on the evening of
February 26 but continued provisionally in office until
March 9 in accordance with the orders of the Emperor.**
The Acting Premier during the first nine days of March was
Admiral Okada whose resignation, submitted to the Emperor
on February 28, was not accepted until the formation of
the Hirota Cabinet on March 9. On March 4 Prince Konoe,
President of the House of Peers, declined the Imperial
Command to form a Cabinet,, alleging ill health as his
reason, and on the following day Mr. Hirota was given
the same command. On March 6 Mr. Hirota tentatively
chose General Count Juichi Terauchi as Minister for
War and between these two men negotiations took place
from March 6 to March 9. Although the newspapers reported
that the Army was opposed to Mr. Hirota as Premier, the
real
See Section III (d) of this report.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1736 of March 18, 1936.

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.99)
-16 -
real hindrance to him in forming a Cabinet was the
objection that he met with on the part of the Army over
certain individuals chosen for posts in the new Cabinet.
According to the press, the Army objected to Mr. Shigeru
Yoshida as Minister. for Foreign Affairs because of his
well-known liberalism and because of his being a son-in-
law of Count Makino, one of the senior statesmen; to Dr.
Hiroshi Shimomura as Overseas Minister because of his
position as Vice President of the ASAHI, which was con-
sidered to be too capitalistic and which had been attacked
by the mutineers on February 26; to Takukichi Kawasaki
as Minister for Education because he was a political
party man; to retention of Mr. Naoshi Ohara as Justice
Minister because he had not been sincere in efforts to
clarify the national polity; and to Mr. Chikuhei Nakajima
as Commerce and Industry Minister because of his interest
in the manufacture of munitions. The Army authorities,
represented by General Terauchi, agreed to cooperate with
Mr. Hirota in the formation of a new Cabinet if the latter
acceded to the four principal demands of the Army, which
were: (1) clarification of the national polity, i. e.
the complete suppression of the Minobe theory and of
liberalism in Japan; (2) an increase in the Army budget
for the building up of a better air force, for the com-
pletion of the mechanization of the Army, and for the
strengthening of the Japanese forces in "Manchukuo" and
at home; (3) stabilization of the livelihood of the people,
that is, economic and political reforms, the establishment
of state control of industries and finance, and readjust-
ment of taxation with an increase of taxes on capitalists
and a decrease on the poorer classes; and (4) the adoption
of a "positive" foreign policy. Although these policies
were

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.99)
-17 -
were labelled "Army demands" by the press generally, it
should be noted that they were supported by large elements
in and out of Government circles who have no affiliations
with the Army. These elements also were opposed to a
certain extent to the appointment of the men to whom the
Army is reported to have objected. Mr. Hirota finally
succeeded in choosine a Cabinet acceptable to the Army.*
Apparently the only point on which he resisted the demands
of the Army was his insistence that the political parties
be substantially represented in the Cabinet. He was
formally installed as Premier by the Emperor on the evening
of March 9 and the complete Cabinet was announced in an
Official Gazette extra of that day as follows: Prime
Minister and Minister for Foreign Affairs, Koki Hirota;
Minister for Home Affairs and concurrently Minister for
Education, Shigenosuke Ushio; Minister for Finance, Eiichi
Baba; Minister for War and President of the Manchurian
Affairs Bureau, General Count Juichi Terauchi; Navy Minister,
Admiral Osami Nagano; Justice Minister, Raisaburo Hayashi;
Minister for Agriculture and Forestry, Toshio Shimada;
Minister for Commerce and Industry, Takukichi Kawasaki;
Communications Minister, Keikichi Tanomogi; Railways
Minister, Yonezo Maeda; and Overseas Minister, Hidejiro
Nagata. Ushio, a member of the House of Peers, had held
a number of important positions in the Home Ministry and
had the reputation of being a very capable administrator.
Baba, a well-known banker and for several years President
of the Hypothec Bank, had no decided affiliations with
any political or financial group and was well versed in
the
* Embassy's despatch No. 1736 of March 18, 1936.

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.99)
- 18 -
the agricultural land situation. General Terauchi was
popular in Army circles and hed the reputation of having
affiliations with no Army faction and with none of the
political parties. Nagano was known for being a highly
capable naval officer, a direct and patriotic negotiator
(he was one of the two delegates to the London Naval
Conference which opened in December, 1935), and a man of
independent leanings from the political point of view.
Hayashi had had a distinguished career in the Ministry
of Justice.* The remaining members of the Cabinet had
not had outstanding careers and were chosen for their
respective posts largely for reasons of expediency and
compromise. The Minseito appointees to the new Cabinet
were Commerce and Industry Minister Kawasaki and Communi-
cations Minister Tanomogi, while the Seiyukai appointees
were Railways Minister Maeda and Minister of Agriculture
and Forestry Shimada. Mr. Hachiro Arita, recently appointed
Ambassador to China, was prominently mentioned by the news-
papers as Mr. Hirota's probable choice for Minister for
Foreign Affairs.
The statement of policy issued by Premier Hirota on
March 17 contained in essence the four basic demands of
the Army which were listed above.**
Furthermore, the statement of policy clearly reflects
the views of the February 26 insurrectionists because
these views were also held by a very considerable part
of the Army and by a large part of the population. The
fundamental desire of the insurgents was a change in the
political status quo. This was brought about.*** Several
of
*
Embassy's telegram No. 65 of March 9, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1733 of March 19, 1936.
***
Embassy's despatch No. 1735 of March 19, 1936.

=== Page 476 === (confidence 1.00)
of the aims of the rebel officers were accomplished and
others appeared definitely to be a part of the new Govern-
ment's program. At the end of the month it could be safely
predicted that the new Government would be more national-
istic than previous Governments, that it would endeavor
to effect many of the economic, political, and social
reforms so eagerly desired by the February 26 mutineers
and by a large part of the Army, and that on the other
hand, no hasty or radical reforms would be adopted.*
Mr. Takukichi Kawasaki, the Minister for Commerce and
Industry, a member of the Minseito and the only hold -over
besides Mr. Hirota in the new Government, died on March 27
and was succeeded by Dr. Gotaro Ogawa, one of the leaders
of the Minseito, a member of the Diet, and Parliamentary
Vice Minister in the Hamaguchi Cabinet. The post of
Education Minister, which had been held concurrently by
Mr. Ushio, Home Minister, was taken over on March 25 by
Dr. Hachisaburo Hirao, a Member of the House of Peers,
President of the Kawasaki Dockyards, and the Chairman of
the Japanese Economic Mission which went to Brazil last year.
Count Yasuya Uchida, who had five times held the
portfolio of Minister for Foreign Affairs and had also
been President of the South Manchuria Railway, died in
Tokyo on March 12 at the age of 71. As Minister for
Foreign Affairs he was known for his chauvinist tendencies.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
In the wake of the February 26 incident, a number
of developments took place during the month of April
which affected the Cabinet. On April 10 the Cabinet
decided to abolish the Cabinet Deliberative Body, which
was organized under the Okada Government and had been
closely associated with the senior statesmen and was
therefore deemed objectionable, in the new order of
things.* It was reported that a plan had been proposed
to reduce the number of Ministers of the various Depart-
ments from the present thirteen to seven and to make them
all Ministers without Portfolio, directly responsible to
the Emperor and entrusted with the formulation of policies
which would be executed by the Ministers with Portfolio.
This proposal met with opposition in the Cabinet, probably
on account of its similarity to the defunct Cabinet
Deliberative Body.**
Mr. Hachiro Arita, Ambassador to China, was formally
installed on April 2 as Minister for Foreign Affairs in
place of Premier Hirota, who had held this post since the
formation of the new Cabinet on March 10. On April 10
Mr. Mamoru Shigemitsu resigned from his post of Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs and was placed on the waiting
list. Although Mr. Shigemitsu had previously been known
as a friend of the military, the press reported that one
of the reasons for his resignation was the Army's opposition
to him. He was succeeded as Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs by Mr. Kensuke Horinouchi, formerly Chief of the
American
Embassy's despatch No. 1735 of March 19, 1936.
Embassy's despatoh No. 1784 of April 17, 1936.

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.99)
- 14 -
American Affairs Bureau of the Foreign Office.
At its meeting on April 10, the Cabinet decided
to reprimand the following officials for their con-
nection with the February 26 incident: Kazuo Oguri,
former Chief of the Metropolitan Police Board; Toshiki
Karasawa, former Director of the Folice Bureau of the
Home Office; Sei Homma, Chief of the Folice Affairs
Department of the Metropolitan Police Board; and Katsu-
roku Aikawa, Chief of the Peace Maintenance Section of
the Home Office. In keeping with the announced policy
of the new Government to clarify the national polity,
the new Minister of Education set forth his views as
follows to a representative of the JAPAN ADVERTISER on
April 29: Japan has for a number of years been adapting
to its needs the modern arts and sciences of the Occident;
in this process greater emphasis has been placed on the
materialistic phase of culture to the detriment of the
spiritual side; Japan is therefore suffering at present
from "unbalance", a situation which must be corrected
through the "humanization of education".
On April 24, the Cabinet decided that meetings
would be held by the Ministers for Foreign Affairs, War,
and Navy every Friday; that discussion would not be
limited at these meetings to specific topics but would
embrace everything relating to diplomacy and national
defense; and that the ultimate purpose of the meetings
would be to attain understanding among the three ministers
concerned for the formulation of national policies. The
Minister for Foreign Affairs explained in this connection
that the purpose of such meetings was the unification of
Japan's foreign policy through complete coordination
between the Foreign Office and the defense services.

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.99)
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinet.
Very little of a definite nature was decided upon by
the Government on the question of social, administrative,
and financial reform, to Which program the Hirote Cabinet
had pledged itself shortly after its formation. On the
other hand, there was no indication that the Government
had in the sligitest degree shaken itself free from the
influence of the Army, which has consistently clemored for
reform ever since the February 26 Incident in return for
the castigation of past offenders and the eradication of
potentially subversive elements in the Army. Political
observers opine that the so-called defense accounts, in
which considerable increases have been announced by the War
and Navy Ministers, will have to be examined and approved
in some form by the Governnent before consideration can be
Civen to increased expenditures for social aid, the proposed
increase in taxes, and the plan for the expansion of state
monopolies.
Considerable

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.99)
-15 -
Considerable attention wes given by the press to
what would come of the resolutions passed at the special
session of the Diet for the setting up of three committees:
one for the reforn of the House of Peers, another for the
reform of the Lower fouse, end the third for the revision
of the Electoral Law. At first the House of Teers as a
whole and its President, Irince Konoye, in particular were
reported to be oposed to the Government's plan for appoint-
ing a committee for the investigation of the reform of this
body. However, it was stated by the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
June 23 that on June 21 Prince Konoye had agreed with
Cabinet officials upon the establishment of such a com-
mittee. On June 12 the Cabinet had already approved the
appointment of aommissions to investigate the reform of the
House of Representatives and of the Electoral Law.
Premier Hirota and his Cabinet were said to have
agreed in principle on the establishment of a system of
Minister without portfolio, which had been advocated by
the Army for the purpose of entrusting such Linisters with
important decisions on national policy and leaving the
regular Cabinet Ministers free to attend to purely ad-
ministrative duties. At the end of the month, however,
it was reported by the newspapers that the Cabinet had
decided against the appointment at present of Ministers
without portfolio.
Addresses were made by various Cabinet members at the
six-day conference of prefectural governors which lasted
from June 15 to June 20. The Premier's principal speech
was vague and met with criticism from the press on account
of its failure to nention any of the details of the
Government's

=== Page 493 === (confidence 0.98)
-15 -
Governnent's promised reforn progrem. Both the Home and
Justice inisters issued wernirs e cinst possible sub-
versive activities, the forner statin that "dark under-

bringing about a "drastic renovation" in the taxation

In short, the addresses of these Cabinet Ministers
serve as an illustration of the apprehensiveness prevailing
after the insurrection of Februery 2s and the uncertainty
of the Covernment's ability to cope with the situation in
the long run. There renains the possibility that while
the Army may prove capable of establishin discipline
within its nidst for the present, of which ample manifesta-
tions have been given*, the Government may by procrastina-
tion lose the confidence of the Army and hence of a large
part of the people.

=== Page 495 === (confidence 0.98)
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
Very little was done by the Government toward
out the policies announced shortly after the Incide
002
February 26 and consequently the Cabinet continued
critlcized by the Army and the newspapers. However
announced by the press that the Cabinet had decided
consider the following "truly important" policies:
*
Embassy's despatch No. 1936 of July 9, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 1953 of July 22, 1936.

=== Page 496 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
1. Increase of defensive equipment, submitted
by the War and Navy Ministries.
2.
Readjustment of taxation, including a tax
increase, submitted by the Finance Ministry.
3.
Nationalization of electric power, submitted
by the Communications Ministry.
4.
Central control of fuel, submitted by the
Commerce and Industry Ministry.
5.
Extension of the tern of compulsory education,
submitted by the Education Ministry.
6.
Revision of the agricultural village and land
system, submitted by the Agriculture and
Forestry Ministry.
It having been decided that the Cabinet as a whole
would consider the administrative and social reforms
proposed by the various ministries, a meeting to this
effect was held on July l0. After this meeting, however,
it was announced that the policy of consideration of the
national policy plans by the Cabinet as a whole had been
abandoned owing to the large number of such plans, friction
among Cabinet members, and dissatisfaction on the part of
the War and Navy Ministries, which wanted priority to be
given to their plans and budgets. Therefore it was decided
that the respective ministries would submit their individual
plans in writing to the Premier and the Finance Minister,
who would later submit to the Cabinet Council those plans
deemed feasible. Apparently Premier Hirota had decided
that it was necessary to allot first the minimum which the
defense services could be induced to accept and later to
consider the plans of the other Ministries in the light
of the money remaining.* It appeared to be fairly obvious
that the defense Ministries, particularly the War Ministry,
were asserting control over the Government. At the same
time the Army continued to express its dissatisfaction
with the Hirota Cabinet.

=== Page 502 === (confidence 0.99)
III. INTERNAL FOLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet.
Although the question of administrative reform con-
tinued to occupy the press, no progress was made in
894.002
clarifying the details of the reform proposal. Premier
Hirota appeared to be giving his personal attention to
the matter in private discussions but avoiding letting the
plan become an open issue.* Toward the end of the month
the newspapers reported that the Army had come forward
with a sugeestion that political reform should include a
restriction of the powers of the Diet whereby Diet members
would not be permitted to become Cabinet ministers and the
political parties would be entirely divorced from the
administration of Government, being allowed only to debate
in the Diet. Opposition to the Army's reported reform
proposal was widespread.**
At a Cabinet meeting held on October 20 a compromise
agreement acceptable to Mr. Tanomogi, Minister for Com-
munications, was approved "in principle" on the much-
discussed
*
Embassy's despatcin No. 2087 of October 16, 1936.
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2117 of October 31, 1936.

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.99)
-15 -
discussed plan for siate control of electric power industries.
While certain alterations were made in Mr. Tanomogi's original
plan, his thesis of "private ownership and state operation"
remained the basis of the compromise plan, the essential
points of which are as follows: that the various privately
owned electric power industries shall be required to buy
shares in a state institution called the Electric Power
Facilities Company; and that the Electric Power Facilities
Company shall, within a limited period of years, employ funds
derived from loans or surplus in buying back at par the
shares of those privately owned electric power companies
which are willing to sell their shares. It was understood
that the plan would be submitted to the Diet after the New
Year holidays and, according to press indications, it should
meet with opposition in that body.*

The Cabinet: The Vice Ministers for War and Navy

Ministers a joint recommendation for adminis-

=== Page 505 === (confidence 0.97)
(a). The Cabinet.
On November 9 the Vice Ministers for War and Navy
submitted .to a conference of four Cabinet Ministers a
joint .recommendation for administrative reform, the chief
qt
points of which were the following: there should be a
6
commission of general inquiry, supervision, and coordina-
tion under the jurisdiction of the Prime Minister; the
head of this commission, whether or not a Cabinet member,
should be authorized at times to attend Cabinet meetings
in the capacity of a Cabinet Minister (this point represented
a concession to the opposition to the Army's original plan
of having a Cabinet Minister without Portfolio as the head
of this office); the Foreign Office end the Ministry of
Overseas Affairs should be combined in the interest of a
unified external policy; the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry and the Ministry of Commerce and Industry should
be combined; foreign trade, fuel policy, electric power,
and other matters should be controlled by a unified office;
aviation, railways, roads, wharves, and all communications
should be under the control of a single Department with
particular stress upon the promotion of civil aviation;
and the Law of the Houses of the Diet, as well as the
Election Law, should be revised for the purpose of renovating
the Diet.*
There was very little discussion in the press of the
Government's plan for state control of electric power
industries
* Embassy's despatch No. 2l4l of November 14, 1936.

=== Page 506 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
industries, the matter apparently being considered closed
until the opening of the next Diet Session.* However, a
joint statement urging the Government to leave the manage-
ment of electric power industries in private hands was
issued by executives of the so-called "Big Five" electric
power companies on November 14.
(b). The Diet.
The proposal put forward by undefined military circles
for restriction of the Powers of the Diet** was received with
widespread and intense opposition. Consequently, the War
Minister on November 6 issued a statement in which he denied
that the Army had any intention of reducing the powers of
the Diet. On the same day General Terauchi told Cabinet
members that the Army's policy of giving expression to
political views only through the War Minister would remein
unchanged and he denied knowledge of the source of the pro-
posal for drastic Diet restriction attributed by the press
to the Army. There were evidences of resentment among some
of the younger and more politically-minded officers of the
Army after the issuance of the War Minister's statement.
As already stated in this report, on November 9 the
Vice Ministers for War and Navy presented a joint statement
for administrative reform to a conference of four Cabinet
Ministers which contained a vague proposal for renovation
of the Diet system. This proposal appeared to be a compro-
mise to appease the younger Army element on one hand, and
to agree with the War Minister's statement of November 6
that the Army did not intend to reduce the powers of the
Diet on the other hand.***

=== Page 550 === (confidence 0.99)
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinet.
The Hirota Cabinet met with severe criticism at the
hands of the political parties and the newspapers, part-
ly on damestic issues but principally on the grounds that
the Sino-Japanese negotiations had failed; that the nego-
tiations for the anti-Comintern pact with Germany had been
conducted with too much secrecy; that the pact itself had
been received with disfavor abroad, probably because the
Government had failed to explain its "true nature" proper-
ly; that Mr. Arita's disclosure of the conclusion of the
anti-

=== Page 551 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
anti-Comintern agreement to the Soviet Ambassador had
been premature and had ceused the USSR to delay the sign-
ing of the revised Fisheries Treaty; and that the Govern-
ment had made no progress in its negotiations with the
Soviet Union over the pro osed sorder commissions.*
How-
ever, there was little or no criticism of the nature of
the agreement with Germany end none whatsoever of the
Japanese militery for havin, forced the Cabinet to at-
tempt to le lize the Aray's actions in North China through
the issuance of demands for an agreement which the Nanking
Governnent could not be oxpectod to sanction.
A temporary respite fron criticism wcs afforded the
Cabinet by Chang Hsueh-liang's attempted coup at Sian.
The Government could argue thnet the Sien rebellion had
been due to communist instigation and hencc justificd
Japan's agreement with Gernany and the t negotiations with
China could .ot be continu d so long as the disturbed
politiccl conditions preveiled in that country. However,
criticism of tho Hirote Government was resumed with force
in the latter half of Deceiber and it appcared at the
close of the month that the Cabinot might have to face
organized opposition from the political partios, the
Houso of Peers, tho pross, and other quarters, espocially
during tho forthcoming session of the Dict. Not only
the Shakai Taishuto, tho minority party which has criti-
cizcd the Government consistontly, but also tho two prin-
cipal political parties (thc Minsoito and the Sciyukai)
werc reportedly dotcrmined to criticizo thc Govornmont
on both forcign and domostic issucs.

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.94)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DCR
1937
March 4, 1937
till
1937 MAR
AM
A FATERN AFFAINS
VISION OF
MMiN. NoNtaké Sato, recently appointed
894.
Japanese Minister for Foreign Affairs
0B
Mr. Sato, who is fifty-seven years old, is the
894.002/323
adopted son of Mr. Aimaro Sato, Japanese Ambassador to
the United States some twenty-five years ago.
He joined the Foreign Service of Japan in 1906, and
has subsequently served almost entirely in Europe, his
successive posts having been Secretary of the Japanese
Embassy at St. Petersburg, Counselor of the Embassy at
Paris, Minister to Poland, Director of the Japanese
League of Nations office at Geneva, Ambassador to
Belgium, and finally Ambassador to France. After the
War, Mr. Sato was closely associated with the League of
2
PTLHID
Nations in various capacities, the last being as Japanes
member of the Council of the League during the Sino-Japa
nese conflict.
Mr. Sato speaks English well and French almost
perfectly. He has a pleasant and agreeable disposition,
and he is -- for a Japanese -- cosmopolitan in his point
of viow. His association over a long period of time with
F/FG
Europeans, especially his extended service at Geneva, has
naturally tended to produce in him a relatively liberal
attitude toward international affairs. He is not a strong
14
personality,

=== Page 553 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
personality, and being of a cautious temperament,it is
unlikely that Mr. Sato will initiate any radical changes
in Japanese foreign policy.
Early this year, before there occurred the recent
change of government in Japan, it was stated in the press
that Mr. Sato had informed his Government of his intention
to retire from the Foreign Service and that his resignation
had been accepted. The announcement yesterday that Mr. Sato
had been appointed Minister for Foreign Affairs, therefore,
occasioned some surprise. It is known that the Prime
Minister, General Hayashi, was unable to induce Ambassador
Saito, at Washington, and one or two of the other younger
ambassadors to consider favorably the post of Minister
for Foreign Affairs, for the reason that there is general
expectation that the Hayashi Cabinet will not be able to
maintain itself for very long. Mr. Sato, however, was in
a different position: he had apparently resolved to
retire from the Foreign Service, and no risk was involved
of sacrificing a career in his acceptance of the post of
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
AD
FEIMID/DLY

=== Page 561 === (confidence 0.98)
(b). The Cabinet.
After the resignation of the Hirota Cabinet, the
Emperor on January 24 requested General Kazunari Ugaki,
894.
002
the well-known liberal, to form a Cabinet.*** Ostensibly
on the ground that the Army would not be capable of en-
forcing discipline with an Ugaki Cabinet in power, the
Army refused to furnish a War Minister for the proposed
Cabinet and therefore on January 29 General Ugaki reported
to the Emperor that he was unable to form a Government.
There was a strong undercurrent of criticism among the
people of the Army's obstruction of General Ugaki, who
undoubtedly was the nation's choice as Premier. According
to the press, Baron Hiranuma, reported to have fascist ten-
dencies, was asked to form a Cabinet but stated that he
was unable to accept the responsibility of the Premiership.
On January 29 General Senjuro Hayashi, retired, War Minister
in the Okada Cabinet until hts resignation on September 4,
1935, was selected to form a Cabinet, which task it was
believed would not be difficult in view of the Army's support
of the Premier-designate.****
(c).

=== Page 587 === (confidence 0.94)
74-1
UNDLR SECH
THE
JUN 4- 1937
DcR
IVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 2, 1937
The selection of Prince Konoye as
JOPrime Minister of Japan.
JUN
NOTED
DIVISION OF
MM BrinencFumimaro Kopoye (Konoe), who has been com-
ANC
manded to form a Cabinet in Japan, is the first and highest
894.002
noble in Japan. The family traces its lineage back to the
"Age of the Gods". With the exception of Prince Saionj1,
894.4
he is the only representative of the pre-Restoration
894.002/
Kinmye
Court Nobility who has been active in Japan's political
life since the Restoration of 1868. It will be recalled
that after the February 26, 1936, Incident, Saionji recom-
mended Konoye as a successor to Okada, whose ministry fell
3
3
after that Incident, but Konoye declined at that time.
8
The fact that Konoye has again been sought for the premier-
ship is of exceptional significance. Not because of any
demonstrated political ability on his part but because of
his great family and personal prestige, which should enable
him to enlist the support of the ablest statesmen, he, if
111N
FILAD
anyone, may be expected to be able to bring the opposing
elements in Japan out of the deadlock which has been
1037
oreated by the inept methods of General Hayashi.
Konoye, who is forty-five years of age, will be the
youngest man who has been Prime Minister of Japan within the
last half century at least. So far he is without experience
in party politics. The positions of Vice President and
F/
President of the House of Peers whioh he has held suocessively
FG
since

=== Page 588 === (confidence 0.97)
74-2
- 2 -
since 193l are traditionally regarded as requiring aloof-
ness from politics. Nevertheless, with the possible
exception of Hirota no recent Prime Minister has been
better trained to deal with affairs from a world point
of view. Konoye was on the staff of Prince Saionji,
the principal Japanese delegate to the Versailles Peace
Conference; he visited the United States in 1934, when he
was received by the President and by the Secretary, and
he had at that time a number of conferences with other
statesmen, publicists and business leaders. Articles
published by him upon his return to Japan and interviews
given by him show that in the course of his American
visit he obtained a good grasp of the American attitude
toward Far Eastern questions. He has a strong tie with
this country in that he has a son at Princeton.
It should not be understood, however, that Konoye's
selection for the premiership represents a triumph for
liberalism. It is true that he himself is inclined toward
liberalism, but his selection as Prime Minister is undoubted-
ly based upon the expectation that he will remain a neutral
in politics. His selection, therefore, represents rather
a compromise, and his Cabinet will probably be a coalition
formed by the retention of the Ministers of War and of the
Navy of the outgoing cabinet and the inolusion of repre-
sentatives of the political parties. While it is not yet
definite that he will succeed in forming a cabinet, there
is little likelihood that he will fail.
S

=== Page 596 === (confidence 1.00)
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a). The Cabinet and the Political Parties.
The Japanese press in general interpreted the results
of the Diet election of April 30 as a defeat for the Hayashi
94.
Cabinet, a victory for the political parties.* Since there
were no clear-cut issues offered by either of the major
political parties, the Seiyukai and the Minseito, the voters
elected much the same class of men to the Diet as those who
had been members when the House was dissolved on March 31,
1937.**
On May 3 the Cabinet issued a statement to the effect
that the Lower House had been dissolved on March 3l because
regeneration of parliamentary government was the cornerstone
of governmental reform; that the Government wanted the newly-
elected Diet members to be guided by a spirit of self-sacrifice
in
*
Embassy's despatch No. 2428 of May 15, and Embassy's
Monthly Political Report for April, Section III (a).
**
Embassy's despatch No. 2423 of May 14, 1937.

=== Page 597 === (confidence 0.99)
-12 -
in developing the constitutional system peculiar to Japan;
that the people shared the Government's desire in this respect;
and that the Government would continue on its way "confident
of the nation's support of the Government's earnest desire
for perfect cooperation with the people."*
Premier Hayashi issued a statement to the effect that
the election returns were not a surprise to the Government
and that the Government had had no intention of attempting
to win the election.
Opposition to the Government by the Seiyukai, the Min-
seito, and the Shakai Taishuto increased during the month,
leaders of these parties issuing statements which assailed
the policies of the Hayashi Cabinet and particularly its
high-handed attitude toward the parties. The political
parties were particularly irate over General Hayashi's
failure to include in his Cabinet a few Ministers taken
from the parties. Finally on May 3l the Hayashi Cabinet
resigned en bloc. The decision of the Cabinet to resign
came soon after the issuance of a vigorous public statement
by Prince Konoe that Premier Hayashi should have chosen some
Ministers from the political parties immediately after the
election of April 30.**
Little or no progress seemed to have been made during
the month toward the establishment of the proposed new
political party, reactionary in character and designed to
support the Hayashi Cabinet or similar non-party cabinets
that might be formed in the future.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinet.
Following the resignation of the Hayashi Cebinet on
May 31 the following Cabinet, under the Premiership of Prince
Konoye, was installed on June 4:
Foreign Minister: Mr. Koki Hirota, member of the House
of Peers, former Pr-mier and Foreign
Minister.
Home Minister:
Dr. Eichi Daba, member of the House
of Peers, former Finance Minister (Hir-
ota Cabinet).
Finance Minister:
Mr. Okonobu Kaya (Vice Minister for
Finance under Finance Minister Yuki
in the Hirota Cabinet).
Minister of War:
General Gen Sugiyama, continuing in
office.
Minister of Navy:
Admiral Mitsumasa Yonai, continuing in
office.
Minister of Education: Mr. Eiji Yasui, Governor of Osaka
Prefecture.
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry: Count Yoriyasu
Arime, member of the House of Peers.
Minister of Commerce and Industry: Mr. Shunji Yoshino,
former Vice Minister for Comnerce and
Industry (Hirota Cebinet).
Minister
* Embassy's telegram No: 171, July 1, 1937.

=== Page 600 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
Minister of Communications:
Mr. Yutaro Nagai, a Min-
seito member of the House
of Representatives, former
Minister for Overseas Affairs.
Minister of Reilways: Mr. Cuikuhei Nakajima, a Seiyukai
member of the House of Representa-
tives.
Minister for Overseas ffairs: Mr. Sonyu Otani, member
of the House of Peers.
Minister of Justice: Mr. Sui iko Shiono, continuing in
office.
On the evenin of June 4 Prince Konoye stated in a radio
address that it would be his policy in foreign relations to
seek international justice, and in domestic relations to seek
social justice. (It was subsequently reported that the Premier
was studying measures for the realization of "peace based on
justice and not on the status quo". If such is the Premier's
plan it would indicate that he intends to further Japan's de-
sire for a redistribution of world markets). Prince Konoye's
radio address did not constitute a political platform. In
fact, his statement that he could not yet announce concrete
points of his platform gave the impression that the policy
of the Cabinet would be to meet questions as they arise rather
than to try to carry out any formulated platform.*
Before accepting the task of formine a Cabinet Prince
Konoye obtained tha consent of the irmy and the Navy to the
continuing in office of General Sugiyama and Admiral Yonai as
Army and Navy Ministers, respectively. This consent was ap-
parently given without conditions as to the composition of the
Cabinet. Therefore, it represented an expression of confidence
in Prince Konoye as Premier rather than an approval of the in-
dividuals to be chosen for each post in the Cabinet.
Japanese
* Embassy's despatch No. 2469, June 11, 1937.

=== Page 601 === (confidence 0.98)
-14-
Japanese businessmen and financiers were openly apprehen-
sive concerning the appointment as Home Minister of Dr. Baba,
former Finance Minister, since they feared that he would be,
as heretofore, an agent of the military element in promoting
plans for the expansion of rigid control over industry and for
expensive schemes which will strain the country's credit and
resourccs.
On June 7 Premier Konoye appointed Mr. Hirota Chairman
of the Plannin Board which lied been established by Imperial
Ordinance on May 13, 1937, auring the incumbency of the Hayashi
Cabinet. The Board is apparontly designed to be a coordinating
agency of Government policy. However, the powers of the Board
as set forth in the Orainance of May 13 are vefue to the point
of obscurity.*
(b) Political Parties.
Although the Konoye Cabinct contains only onc Minister
from the Seiyukai and only one Minister from the Minseito, the
two parties appeared to bo satisficd with the Cabinet, partly
because of Premier Konoye's accision not to attach any con-
ditions to tho appointment of thc Seiyukei and Minseito Min-
isters. On the dey of installation of the new Cabinet the
joint Minscito-Seiyukai committce for the overthrow of the
Hayashi Cabinet cisbanded. Furthcrmore, the two perties
agreed that whilc the new Cabinct's stand regarding the po-
litical parties wes regrettablc in somc rospects, the pertics
should eive the Cabinet their morel support.
Accordine to the JAPIN DVERTISER of June 22, the Politi-
cel Rcform Council,  rightist organization, has decided to
abandon its plan to organize c strong politiccl party includ-
ing all roform clomonts, owing to the strongth of the reform
movemont
* Embcssy s despetch No. 2466, June 10, 1937.

=== Page 602 === (confidence 0.99)
-15-
movement in the Minseito and Seiyukai parties. This news-
paper stated that the rightist organization in question
would concentrate on the formation of a group in the Lower
House, with right-wing independents as its nucleus, to off-
set the influence of the Social Mass Party.
(c). The Diet.
The apparent approval of the Cabinet by the Seiyukai
and the Minseitc would indicate that the two major parties
do not intend to be obstructive in the next session of the
newly elected House of Repres ntatives.* The Diet has been
ordered to convene on July 23 and to be in session for 14
days.
(a). The Army.
According to the TOKYO ICHI NICnI of June 3 the "Big
Three" of the Army (the War Minister, the Chief of the Gen-
eral Staff, and the Inspector (eneral of Military Education)
consented to the appointment of General Sugiyama as War Min-
ister in the Konoye Cabinet in consideration of the follow-
ing four items of policy to be fulfilled by the new Cabinet:
1) clarification of the national polity; 2) repletion of
national defense; 3) stability of the living of the people;
and 4) reform of the Diet. It will be remembered that these
aims have been openly sponsored by the Army ever since the
Incident of February 26, 1936.
In view of the shortage of officers in the Japanese
Army, the War Office has decided to increase considerably
not only the number of cadets in the Military Academy but
also the number of recruits in the Military Preparatory
Academy. The Army also disclosed that the age of recruits
for the Military Preparatory Academy would be raised from
16
* Embassy's despatch No. 2469, June 11, 1937.

-16-
16 to 20 to from 16 to 22.
(e). House of Peers.
Prince Konoye has been known for some time to be in
favor of reforming the House of Peers. He was said to have
told newspaper reporters on June 20 that in his opinion a
reform of the Upper House should first of all include a re-
duction of the proportion of Peers to the total membership
of the Upper House; that the Lower House represented public
opinion and could thus be called the House of common sense;
and that the House of Peers should have more men of expert
knowledge and experience and be reorganized so that it would
stand for "expert opinions and not for the Peers". It was
reported by the press that even the progressive members of the
House of Peers were "astounded" at Premier Konoye's views.

effect cabinet ministers without portfolio,
serving on account of the emergency situation.
The men appointed are of better than ordinary
inet
rank and have been selected with care from
the more important fields of Japanese activi-

against a progressive. The move has definite
utility in preparation for generel acceptance
of whetever may be the terms of peace, show-

peace negotiated with kussie in 1905 has not
been forgotten. The conservatives among these

sugu, and Baron Go. The progressives include
General Ugaki, Admiral Baron Abo, and Mr.
Ikeda. Mr. Matsuoka is in some respects a
progressive and in others a reactionary.
