Sir:
have the honor to report the appointment on
A-M/C
15, 1937 of ten temporary counselors to the Japanese
(rinji naikaku sangi). The new counselors are in
efft cabinet ministers without portfolio, serving on ac
couft'of the emergency brought on by the hostilities in
1977
F/A
China.
The office of temporary cabinet counselor is created
by ordinance 593 promulgated on October 14, 1937, and ef-
fective from that date. The ordinance briefly states that
counselors shall have the status of cabinet ministers, and
that they are named by the Emperor to participate in plan-
ning by the Cabinet in important matters with regard to the
China incident. Their number is not stipulated.
It should be observed that the counselors are not
set up as a separate emergency council. For a long time
11
the

=== Page 610 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
the publio has been accustomed to hear proposals for the
creation of new planning and governing bodies, which have
usually been motivated by the desire to circumvent the
existing political organs. The present change, on the other
hand, has the virtue of not complicating the structure of
government while at the same time allowing for the audition
of outside statesmen to the administration's forces.
On October 15 the official gazette announced the
appointment of the following ten men to the newly created
office:
General Kazunari Ugaki, formerly governor
general of Chosen, unsuccessful by reason of
military opposition when named to form a
Japanese cabinet in January 1937.
Chuji Machida, president of the Minseito
party, formerly minister of commerce and industry.
Baron Kiyotane Abo, admiral, formerly minister
1
of the navy.
Baron Sadao Araki, general, formerly minister
021
of war, leader of the extreme military party
following the Manchuria incident of 1931.
Admiral Nobumaso Suetsugu, formerly member of
021
the supreme war council.
OBF
Yonezo Maeda, formerly minister of commerce
and industry.
Kiyoshi Akita, formerly Seiyukai speaker of
0F1
the house of representatives.
Yosuke Matsuoka, president of the South
CH
Manchuria Railway.
NBF
Baron Seinosuke Go, formerly president of
the Tokyo chamber of commerce.
OBP
Seihin Ikeda, formerly governor of the
Bank of Japan.
These men are of better than cabinet rank, as the
rank of men of cabinet caliber goes in ordinary peace times.
They have been selected with care from the most important
fields of Japanese activity, more or less in pairs, a con-
servative against a man of more advanced views. Baron Go
Vis a conservative business man, Mr. Ikeda a liberal business
man

=== Page 611 === (confidence 0.94)
-3-
man. General Araki is of the aggressive army group,
General Ugaki strongly opposes that group. Admiral Suetsugù
and Admiral Abo respectively represent two quite different
types of sentiment in the navy. Military representation is
heavy but not overpowering.
The addition of these counselors brings to the Cabinet
not only the advantae of the judgment of experienced leaders
but also the fuller support of the groups which they repre-
sent. Furthermore, the move has definite utility in prepara-
tion for general acceptance of whatever may be the terms of
peace to conclude the present hostilities. This aspect has
been noted, in fact, by several of the newspapers. The
violent reaction in Japan to the peace negotiated with Russia
by Komura in 1905 has not been forgotten. This time the
ground will be prepared, and tne broad representation brought
to the Cabinet by the new counselors can be counted upon to
be an important factor in the process.

=== Page 672 === (confidence 0.99)
The debate on the formulation of plans for the establishment
of a political institution or central organ for the development,
control, and execution of all affairs involving Japan and C hina has
been the most upsetting influence in the Cabinet since its forma-
tion under Premier Konoe. To the difference of opinions over this
major questicn has been attributed the resignation of former Foreign
Minister Koki Hirota and the reorganication ef the Cabinet in the
spring of this year. At least a dozen different times during the
month of September there wore headlines in the pruss concerning
compromises, revisions, and deadlocks in the controversy. This
was climaxed suddenly and untiruly unexpectedly by the resignation
of Foreign Minister Ugnki on the t wenty-ninth of Septcmber.
The
* Embassy's telegram No. 611, September 20, 1938.

=== Page 673 === (confidence 0.97)
-2o
The original plen, according to the ASAHI, was for a board to
take over the control of the administration of China affairs gn-
erally, economic, financi:l, cultural, diplomatic, etc., ard tu?
supervision  the North China Dev lopment Compary cnd th, Cuntiel
China Promotion Compony in ddition to nanaling all matters relatin
to Japan's policy towards China.
Although completely uthoritative inform tion is un v iloble
it is believed that the Foreign Minister's objections, in addition
to being based on lug l or constitutional grounde, was th t a board
or political institute of such scope was unnecussary at the present
time end particularly was unnecessary as  purmanent addition to
Japan's political structurc. It is obvious also that such un in-
stitution would preempt many of the prerogatives of the Foreign
Office in the conduct of relations between Japan end Chinn and
between Japan and third Powers with interests in China. Immediate-
1y prior to the Foreign Minister's resignation, however, it was
stated in the press that subordinates of the various Ministries
had been successful in reaching a compromise which was to be ap-
proved the following day.
The vernacular press was practically unanimous in its expres-
sion of regret nnd disappointment for General Ugaki's resignation and
there was considerable censure of his action taken at a time when
unity in political affairs is most urgently desired. The TOkYo
NICHI NICHI went so far as to point to the inspiring example of the
statemanship and spirit of conciliation and cooperation evidenced
by the European Powers in their recent crisis, as an example, em-
phasizing the contrast showing a lack of conciliation and coopera-
tion in the Konoe Cabinet as evidenced by the resignation of Foreign
Minister Kazushige Ugaki.

concurrently since the resignation of General Ugaki last month.
The selection of Mr. Arita as Foreign Minister gave no direct
information on the question whether there was to be any change
in the policy of Japan toward the Nestern Powers,** Mr. Arita's
ceptance of the portfolio would seem to inuicate that he supports
fully the Japanese "immutable" policy as regards China and it
is to be expected that he will press the various nations to
recognize the "new situation in the Far East." Mr. Arita's par-
ticipation in the Cabinet does not restore the prestige lost
through former Minister Ugaki's resignation, but it does relieve
the Premier of some of his extra duties. The official statement
of the Government which was to be issued on November 3, it was
hoped, would throw further light on the future policy of Japan
in its relations with the various Powers.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) The Cabinet.
The Cabinet of which Frince Fumimaro Konoye was Premier
resigned on January 4 and on January 5 a Cabinet under the
premiership of Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma was installed.
With the principal exceptions of Mr. Ikeda, who held con-
currently the office f Minicter of Finance and Minister
of Commerce, and Admiral Suetsugu, who held the office
of Home Minister, the outstanding fi, ures of the Konoye
Cabinet were carried over into the new one. These were:
Marquis Kido, General Itagaki, Admiral Yonai, Mr. rita,
General Araki, and Mr. Shiono. Prince Konoye himself was
designated Minister without Portfolio in the Hiranuma Cabinet
while at the same time he became Baron Hiranuma's successor
as President of the Privy Council. The new Cabinet installed
on January 5 was made up as follows:
Prime Minister
Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma
Minister without Portfolio
Prince Fumimaro Konoye
Minister for Foreign Affairs
Mr. Hacliro Arita
Minister of War

Admiral Mitsumasa Yonai

Mr. Yoshiaki Hatta

Mr. Yukio Sakurauchi
Forestry
Minister of Education
General Baron Sadao Araki

Welfare Minister
Mr. Hisatada Hirose

knowledge of the causes that brought such change about and

the political situation prior to and following the change in-

of Commerce and Industry, War and Navy regarding matters relating
to war-time cconomic dministration.
The practicol import of these changes will be that of con-
solidating and placing on a rel tively permanent basis measures
that have alrendy been undertaken by the Government more or less
of a tomporary choracter, and thet of implementing further the
Government's policy of control :nd regimentation of the economic
life of the nation that has attended the emergency situation in
Japan since the outbrenk of the Chinn Incident.*

MR. HORNHECK

SEP 231609
M
Mr. Secrete
DIVI
If it is true that Admiral Nomura has been
offered the post of Minister for Foreign Af-
fairs, we will have another illustration of the
extent to which the Japanese coordinate policy
and agencies and instruments for the carrying
out of policy. The Japanese State is today a
militarized and military machine. With a
General as Premier, a General as head of the
War Department, an Admiral as head of the Navy
Department, and an Admiral as head of the
Foreign Ofrice -- they can at least make their
policy of pursuing national objectives on the
basis of armed force reasonably well coordinat-
ed and potentially effoctive in the field of
expoution.
SKX
1939
Stanley K. Hornbeok

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 19, 1930.
S:
Mr. Secretary.
With reference to Tokyo's telegram no. 485 of
September 19, 5 p.m., stating that, acoording to a re-
port, Admiral Nomura has been offered the post of
Minister for Foreign Affairs, the following biographie
data may be of interest:
Admiral Kichisaburo Nomura, now retired, was born
in 1877. In his career he has served in Austria and
Germany, was a member of the Japanese Delegation to the
Peace Conference after the World War, served as Naval
894.00
Attaohé at Washington (1921), attended the Washington
Maval Conference, was Vioe Chief of the Naval General
Staff, and was a member of the Supreme War Counoil.
Admiral Nomura was Commander of the Japanese naval
foroes at Shanghai during the Sino-Japanese hostilities
of l933, at whioh time he lost an eye as a result of a
bomb thrown by a revolutionary at a gathering of
important Japanese.
Admiral Nomura is an intelligent and able man with
statesmanlike qualities. He has leng been regarded as
OCT:17 1939
favorably disposed toward the United States and toward
Amerioans.

a 2. The new Minister of Navy, Admiral

SUBJECT
Resignation of Prime Minister Abe requested by resolution,
signed by 250 members of the Diet.
Action considered by Embassy as, definitely, a reflection of
the growing popular discontent against the inoreasing goverm-
mental control over all aspects of national activity. With
reference to possible replacement, states it is reasonable
to assume that, if there is not a change of government during
present recess, such change is not expected to occur until
end of session. Sets forth certain qualities that must be
present in the make up of possible successor, to measure up to
popular conception.
